The categorical contours of the Chomsky-Schützenberger representation theorem
Paul-André Melliès ORCID:0000-0001-6180-2275, Noam Zeilberger ORCID:0000-0002-5945-4184

Abstract
. We develop fibrational perspectives on context-free grammars and on nondeterministic finite-state automata over categories and operads. A generalized CFG is a functor from a free colored operad (aka multicategory) generated by a pointed finite species into an arbitrary base operad: this encompasses classical CFGs by taking the base to be a certain operad constructed from a free monoid, as an instance of a more general construction of an operad of spliced arrows 𝒲 ​ 𝒞 \mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}} for any category 𝒞 \mathcal{C} . A generalized NFA is a functor from an arbitrary bipointed category or pointed operad satisfying the unique lifting of factorizations and finite fiber properties: this encompasses classical word automata and tree automata without ϵ \epsilon -transitions, but also automata over non-free categories and operads. We show that generalized context-free and regular languages satisfy suitable generalizations of many of the usual closure properties, and in particular we give a simple conceptual proof that context-free languages are closed under intersection with regular languages. Finally, we observe that the splicing functor 𝒲 : Cat → Oper \mathcal{W}:\mathrm{Cat}\to\mathrm{Oper} admits a left adjoint 𝒞 : Oper → Cat \mathcal{C}:\mathrm{Oper}\to\mathrm{Cat} , which we call the contour category construction since the arrows of 𝒞 ​ 𝒪 \mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{O}} have a geometric interpretation as oriented contours of operations of 𝒪 \mathcal{O} . A direct consequence of the contour / splicing adjunction is that every pointed finite species induces a universal CFG generating a language of tree contour words. This leads us to a generalization of the Chomsky-Schützenberger Representation Theorem, establishing that a subset of a homset L ⊆ 𝒞 ⁡ ( A , B ) L\subseteq\mathcal{C}(A,B) is a CFL of arrows if and only if it is a functorial image of the intersection of a 𝒞 \mathcal{C} -chromatic tree contour language with a regular language.

\titlecomment

This is a thoroughly revised and expanded version of a paper with a similar title
[
MZ22
]
presented at MFPS 2022.
The authors are partially supported by ANR projects CoREACT (ANR-22-CE48-0015), LambdaComb (ANR-21-CE48-0017), and ReciProg (ANR-21-CE48-0019).

†
†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 21, Issue 2, Paper 12

Introduction

In “Functors are Type Refinement Systems”
[
MZ15
]
, we argued for the idea that rather than being modelled merely as categories, type systems
should be modelled as functors
p:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{T}
from a category
\mathcal{D}
whose morphisms may be considered as abstract typing derivations
to a category
\mathcal{T}
whose morphisms are the terms corresponding to the underlying
subjects
of those derivations.
One consequence of this fibrational point of view is that the notion of typing judgment receives a simple mathematical status, as a triple
(R,f,S)

consisting of two objects
R,S
in
\mathcal{D}
and a morphism
f
in
{\mathcal{T}}
such that
p(R)=\mathrm{dom}(f)
and
p(S)=\mathrm{cod}(f)
.
The question of finding a derivation for such a judgment then reduces
to the “lifting problem” of finding a morphism
\alpha:R\to S
such that
p(\alpha)=f
.
Note that this problem could in general have zero, one, or more solutions.

We developed this perspective in a series of papers
[
MZ13
,
MZ15
,
MZ16
,
MZ18
]
, and believe that it may be usefully applied to a large variety of deductive systems, beyond type systems in the traditional sense.
In this work, we focus on derivability in context-free grammars and on recognition by nondeterministic finite-state automata – two classic topics in formal language theory with wide applications in computer science.
Although grammars and automata are of a very different nature, both may be naturally described as certain kinds of functors.

Free operads
1
1

1

In this paper, when we say “operad” we always mean
colored operad
, in the sense that our operads always carry a (potentially trivial) set of colors and operations are sorted
f:A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\to B
in addition to having an arity
n
. An operad in this sense is also called a
multicategory
, and we use the two words interchangeably.
play an important role in our treatment of context-free grammars.
Indeed, we will ultimately propose that a “generalized CFG” may be defined simply as

a functor from a free operad generated by a pointed finite species
into an arbitrary base operad.
In order to recover the classical definition of CFG, we rely on a construction
\mathcal{W}:\mathrm{Cat}\to\mathrm{Oper}
turning any category
\mathcal{C}
into an operad
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
, which we call its
operad of spliced arrows
.
One recovers the classical definition by taking
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
to be the one-object category freely generated from the one-node “bouquet” graph with a loop
a:*\to*
for every letter of the alphabet
a\in\Sigma
.
However, as we will see, it is very natural to consider context-free languages of arrows in an arbitrary category, or even generalized context-free languages of constants in an arbitrary operad.

Nondeterministic finite-state automata, it turns out, may be naturally modelled as
functors satisfying the unique lifting of factorizations and finite fiber properties
.
One nice aspect of this formulation is that it captures both word automata and tree automata, which correspond to functors satisfying these two properties into free categories and into free operads respectively.
However, again we will see that it is very natural and at times essential to work with a more general definition of NFA over a non-free category or operad.

A benefit of expressing both context-free grammars and nondeterministic finite-state automata as functors is that they are placed within a common framework, which facilitates comparison and combination.
The germ of this paper was planted early on in the wake of our type refinement systems work, in 2017, when we began considering context-free grammars as functors of multicategories.
But ultimately it was only in 2022 that the key mathematical definitions emerged very quickly (over the span of a couple weeks), when we mused that it could be interesting to analyze the Chomsky-Schützenberger Representation Theorem,
a classic result in formal language theory that in its now-standard formulation
states that a language is context-free if and only if it is a homomorphic image of the intersection of a Dyck language of well-bracketed words with a regular language.
One reason the theorem is interesting is that it invokes a non-trivial closure property of context-free languages, namely closure under intersection with regular languages.
Another reason is that it suggests, intuitively, that Dyck languages are in some sense “universal” context-free languages.

We will see that both of these aspects of the theorem have good categorical explanations.
First, the intersection of a context-free language with a regular language may be computed by first taking the
pullback
of the functor representing the corresponding context-free grammar along the corresponding nondeterministic finite-state automaton, which for non-trivial but elementary reasons defines a context-free grammar over the
runs
of the automaton.
Second, the aforementioned “spliced arrows” construction
\mathcal{W}
extends to an
adjunction
between the category of categories and the category of operads,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Oper}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}}

where the left adjoint builds what we call the “contour category”
\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{O}}
of an operad
\mathcal{O}
.
This contour / splicing adjunction, which seems to be of independent interest, has as an immediate consequence that every pointed finite species induces a universal CFG generating a language of
tree contour words.

Such contour words are closely related to Dyck words, and allow us to prove a generalization of the representation theorem for context-free languages of arrows in an arbitrary category.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
Section
1
is devoted to context-free grammars, Section
2
to nondeterministic finite-state automata, and Section
3
to the representation theorem.
We discuss related work at the end of each of these sections, and we conclude in Section
4
.

1.
Context-free languages of arrows in a category

In this section, after some preliminaries on free operads and species, we describe the operad of spliced arrows construction, explain how to use it to define context-free grammars over arbitrary categories generating
context-free languages of arrows,
and show that many standard properties of CFGs and CFLs may be naturally reformulated in this general setting.
We also spend some time developing the fibrational view of parsing as a lifting problem, explaining how any functor from a free operad
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{O}
may be represented as a “displayed free operad” corresponding to a lax functor
\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
that admits a certain inductive definition.
Finally, we describe how the definition of categorical CFG admits a further natural generalization by allowing the base operad to be arbitrary, and show that this encompasses several extensions of the notion of context-free grammar from the literature.

1.1.
Preliminaries on free operads and species

We assume some basic familiarity with operads, otherwise known as multicategories, as introduced for example in Chapter 2 of Leinster’s book
[
Lei04
]
.
We write
f\circ(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})
for parallel composition of operations, and
f\circ_{i}g
for partial composition
after
the first
i
inputs (so
f\circ_{0}g
denotes composition of
g
into the 0th input of
f
).
Here we recall the notion of a (colored non-symmetric) species, and how one gives rise to a free operad.

{defi}

A
colored non-symmetric species,
which we abbreviate to
species
for short, is a tuple
\mathbb{S}=(C,V,i,o)
consisting of a span of sets

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o}

with the following interpretation:
C
is a set of
colors,

V
is a set of
nodes,
and
i:V\to C^{*}
and
o:V\to C
return respectively the list of input colors and the unique output color of each node.

Species in this sense could also be called “signatures”, and are sometimes called “multigraphs”
[
Lam89
,
Wal89
]
since they bear the same relationship to multicategories as graphs do to categories (though that terminology has an unfortunate clash with a different concept in graph theory); we use “species” to emphasize the link with Joyal’s theory of (symmetric) species
[
Joy81
]
and with generalized species
[
FGHW08
]
.
Adopting the same notation as we use for operations of an operad, we write
x:R_{1},\dots,R_{n}\to R
to indicate that
x\in V
is a node with list of input colors
i(x)=(R_{1},\dots,R_{n})
and output color
o(x)=R
.
However, it should be emphasized that a species by itself only contains bare coloring information about the nodes, and does not say how to compose them as operations.
We will be primarily interested in
finite
species.

{defi}

We say that a species is
finite
(also called
polynomial

[
Joy86
]
) just in case both sets
C
and
V
are finite.

A map of species
\phi:\mathbb{S}\to\mathbb{T}
from
\mathbb{S}=(C,V,i,o)
to
\mathbb{T}=(D,W,i^{\prime},o^{\prime})
is given by a pair
\phi=(\phi_{C},\phi_{V})
of functions
\phi_{C}:C\to D
and
\phi_{V}:V\to W
making the diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{C}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o^{\prime}}

Equivalently, writing
\phi
for both
\phi_{C}
and
\phi_{V}
, every node
x:R_{1},\dots,R_{n}\to R
of
\mathbb{S}
must be sent to a node
\phi(x):\phi(R_{1}),\dots,\phi(R_{n})\to\phi(R)
of
\mathbb{T}
.
Just as every category has an underlying graph, every operad
\mathcal{O}
has an underlying species with the same colors and whose nodes are the operations of
\mathcal{O}
, and this extends to a forgetful functor
\mathbb{U}:\mathrm{Oper}\to\mathrm{Spec}
from the category of operads and functors of operads to the category of species and maps of species.
Moreover, this forgetful functor has a left adjoint

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spec}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Oper}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{U}}

which sends any species
\mathbb{S}
to a free operad with the same set of colors and whose operations are generated from the nodes of
\mathbb{S}
, analogous to the free category over a graph.
By the universal property of the adjoint pair, there is a natural isomorphism of homsets

\mathrm{Oper}(\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S},\mathcal{O})\cong\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{S},{\mathbb{U}}{\mathcal{O}})

placing functors of operads
p:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{O}
and maps of species
\phi:\mathbb{S}\to{\mathbb{U}}{\mathcal{O}}
in one-to-one correspondence.
In the sequel, we will leave the action of the forgetful functor implicit, writing
\mathcal{O}
for both an operad and its underlying species
{\mathbb{U}}\mathcal{O}
.

1.2.
The operad of spliced arrows of a category

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category.
The
operad
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
of spliced arrows in
\mathcal{C}
is defined as follows:

•

its colors are pairs
(A,B)
of objects of
\mathcal{C}
;

•

its
n
-ary operations
(A_{1},B_{1}),\dots,(A_{n},B_{n})\to(A,B)
consist of sequences
w_{0}{-}w_{1}{-}\dots{-}w_{n}
of
n+1
arrows in
\mathcal{C}
separated by
n
gaps denoted by “
-
”s, where each arrow must have type
w_{i}:B_{i}\to A_{i+1}
for
0\leq i\leq n
, under the convention that
B_{0}=A
and
A_{n+1}=B
;

•

composition of spliced arrows is performed by “splicing into the gaps”: formally, the partial composition
f\circ_{i}g
of a spliced arrow
g=u_{0}{-}\dots{-}u_{m}
into another spliced arrow
f=w_{0}{-}\dots{-}w_{n}
is defined by substituting
g
for the
i
th occurrence of
-
in
f
(starting from the left using 0-indexing) and interpreting juxtaposition by sequential composition in
\mathcal{C}
(see Fig.
1
for an illustration);

•

the identity operation on
(A,B)
is given by
id_{A}{-}id_{B}
.

Figure 1.
Left: a constant of
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
. Middle: an identity operation. Right: example of partial composition, plugging
g=u_{0}{-}u_{1}{-}u_{2}:(C_{1},D_{1}),(C_{2},D_{2})\to(A_{2},B_{2})

into gap 1 of
f=w_{0}{-}w_{1}{-}w_{2}{-}w_{3}:(A_{1},B_{1}),(A_{2},B_{2}),(A_{3},B_{3})\to(A,B)
to obtain
f\circ_{1}g=w_{0}{-}w_{1}u_{0}{-}u_{1}{-}u_{2}w_{2}{-}w_{3}:(A_{1},B_{1}),(C_{1},D_{1}),(C_{2},D_{2}),(A_{3},B_{3})\to(A,B)
.

It is routine to check that
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
satisfies the associativity and neutrality axioms of an operad, these reducing to associativity and neutrality of composition of arrows in
\mathcal{C}
.
Indeed, the spliced arrows operad construction defines a functor

\mathcal{W}:\mathrm{Cat}\to\mathrm{Oper}

since any functor of categories
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
induces a functor of operads

\mathcal{W}\,{F}:\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{D}}
,
acting on colors by
(A,B)\mapsto(FA,FB)
and on operations by

w_{0}{-}\dots{-}w_{n}\mapsto Fw_{0}{-}\dots{-}Fw_{n}
.

{exa}

As referenced in the Introduction, for any alphabet
\Sigma
there is a one-object category
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
, freely generated from the bouquet graph with a loop
a:*\to*
for every letter of
a\in\Sigma
, so that words
w\in\Sigma^{*}
may be regarded as arrows
w:\ast\to\ast
in
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
.
In this case, the construction yields an operad
\mathcal{W}\,{\Sigma}:=\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}}
that we like to call the
operad of spliced words
in
\Sigma
.
It has a single color, and its
n
-ary operations are sequences
w_{0}{-}\dots{-}w_{n}
of
n+1
words over
\Sigma
.
For example, in the operad of spliced words over the ASCII alphabet, we have that

\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}"Hell"}{-}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}", "}{-}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}"rld!"}\circ(\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}"o"},\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}"Wo"})=\texttt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}"Hello, World!"}
.

Remark 1
.

Although
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
is a free category, this property of being freely generated does not extend to its operad of spliced words.
Indeed, an operad of spliced arrows
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
is almost
never
a free operad.
That is because any pair of objects
A
and
B
induces a binary operation
id_{A}{-}id_{A}{-}id_{B}:(A,A),(A,B)\to(A,B)
,
and any arrow
w:A\to B
of
\mathcal{C}
induces a corresponding constant
w:(A,B)
.
Since
id_{A}{-}id_{A}{-}id_{B}\circ(id_{A},w)=w
,
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
cannot be a free operad except in the trivial case where
\mathcal{C}
has no objects and no arrows.

{exa}

Arrows in free categories with more than one object may be seen as “typed” words, in the sense that concatenation (= composition) is only a partial operation that combines a word
u:A\to B
with a word of compatible type
v:B\to C
to produce a word
uv:A\to C
.
For example, consider the graph

obtained by extending the bouquet graph
\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}
into a “cylinder”.
Arrows
w:\ast\to\ast
of
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}^{\textrm{\^{}}\$}}
correspond to ordinary words, which may be concatenated freely, but arrows
u:\bot\to\ast
may only be prepended to the beginning of a word, while arrows
v:\ast\to\top
may only be appended to the end of a word.
As we will see in examples later on, it becomes interesting to consider the spliced arrow operad over
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}^{\textrm{\^{}}\$}}
, which includes operations of the form

f=w_{0}{-}\dots{-}w_{n}\$:(\ast,\ast),\dots,(\ast,\ast)\to(\ast,\top)
and
g=\textrm{\^{}}w_{0}{-}\dots{-}w_{n}:(\ast,\ast),\dots,(\ast,\ast)\to(\bot,\ast)
that may be seen as spliced words with explicit “end of input” and “beginning of input” markers.

Remark 2
.

The operad
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathbf{1}}
of spliced arrows over the terminal category is isomorphic to the terminal operad, with a single color
(\ast,\ast)
, and a single
n
-ary operation
id{-}\dots{-}id:(\ast,\ast),\dots,(\ast,\ast)\to(\ast,\ast)
of every arity
n
.
Likewise, the operad of spliced arrows over the product of two categories decomposes as a product of spliced arrow operads
\mathcal{W}\,{(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{D})}\cong\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{D}}
.
This might suggest that the functor
\mathcal{W}:\mathrm{Cat}\to\mathrm{Oper}
is a right adjoint, and we will see in §
3.2
that this is indeed the case.

1.3.
Context-free grammars over a category

Classically, a context-free grammar is defined as a tuple
G=(\Sigma,N,S,P)
consisting of
a finite set
\Sigma
of terminal symbols,
a finite set
N
of nonterminal symbols,
a distinguished nonterminal
S\in N
called the start symbol,
and a finite set
P
of production rules of the form
R\to\sigma
where
R\in N

and
\sigma\in(N\cup\Sigma)^{\ast}
is a string of terminal or nonterminal symbols.
Observe that any sequence
\sigma
on the right-hand side of a production can be factored as
\sigma=w_{0}R_{1}w_{1}\dots R_{n}w_{n}
where

w_{0},\dots,w_{n}
are words of terminals and
R_{1},\dots,R_{n}
are nonterminal symbols, thus yielding

R\to w_{0}R_{1}w_{1}\dots R_{n}w_{n}

as the general form of a production rule.
We can use this simple observation that production rules factor into nonterminal and terminal components to capture derivations in context-free grammars by functors
p:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\Sigma}
, as illustrated in Figure
2
, and more generally we can use it to define context-free grammars over arbitrary categories.

Figure 2.
Example of a context-free grammar represented by a functor
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\Sigma}
, where we have indicated the action of the functor on the generators as well as the induced action on a closed derivation tree.

{defi}

A
context-free grammar over a category
(or
categorical CFG
) is a tuple
G=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S},S,p)
consisting of a category
\mathcal{C}
, a finite species
\mathbb{S}
equipped with a distinguished color
S\in\mathbb{S}
, and a
functor of operads
p:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
.
We then refer to the colors of
\mathbb{S}
as
nonterminals
and to the operations of
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
as
derivations
or
parse trees
.
The
context-free language of arrows

\mathcal{L}_{G}
generated by the grammar
G
is the subset of
arrows in
\mathcal{C}
which, seen as constants of
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
, are in the image of a closed derivation of
S
, that is,
\mathcal{L}_{G}=\{\,{p(\alpha)\mid\alpha:S}\,\}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(A,B)
, where
p(S)=(A,B)
.

As suggested in the Introduction, every context-free grammar in the classical sense
G=(\Sigma,N,S,P)
corresponds to a context-free grammar over
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
.
For the grammar of Figure
2
, the corresponding species
\mathbb{S}
has three colors
\mathsf{NP},\mathsf{VP},\mathsf{S}
and four nodes,

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle:\mathsf{NP},\mathsf{VP}\to\mathsf{S}

\displaystyle x_{2},x_{3}

\displaystyle:\mathsf{NP}

\displaystyle x_{4}

\displaystyle:\mathsf{NP}\to\mathsf{VP}

with the functor
p:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\Sigma}

(=\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}})
uniquely defined by the action

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle\mapsto\varepsilon{-}\text{\textvisiblespace}{-}\varepsilon

\displaystyle x_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathsf{mom}

\displaystyle x_{3}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathsf{tom}

\displaystyle x_{4}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathsf{loves}\text{\textvisiblespace}{-}\varepsilon

on the generators as displayed in the middle of the figure.
Conversely, any finite species
\mathbb{S}
equipped with a color
S\in\mathbb{S}
and a functor of operads
p:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\Sigma}
uniquely determines a context-free grammar over the alphabet
\Sigma
.
Indeed, the colors of
\mathbb{S}
give the nonterminals of the grammar and
S
the distinguished start symbol, while the nodes of
\mathbb{S}
together with the functor
p
give the production rules, with each node
x:R_{1},\dots,R_{n}\to R
such that
p(x)=w_{0}{-}\dots{-}w_{n}
determining a context-free production rule
x:R\to w_{0}R_{1}w_{1}\dots R_{n}w_{n}
.

Proposition 3
.

A language
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
is context-free in the classical sense if and only if it is the language of arrows of a context-free grammar over
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
.

An interesting feature of the general notion of categorical context-free grammar is that the nonterminals of the grammar
G=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S},S,p)
are
sorted
in the sense that every color of
\mathbb{S}
is mapped by
p
to a unique color of
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
, corresponding to a pair of objects of
\mathcal{C}
.
Adapting the conventions from our work on type refinement systems, we sometimes write
R\ThisStyle{\sqsubset^{p}}(A,B)
or simply
R\ThisStyle{\sqsubset}(A,B)
to indicate that
p(R)=(A,B)
and say that the nonterminal
R
refines the “gap type”
(A,B)
.
We emphasize that the language
\mathcal{L}_{G}
generated by a grammar with start symbol
S\ThisStyle{\sqsubset}(A,B)
is a subset of the homset
\mathcal{C}(A,B)
.

{exa}

To illustrate some of the versatility afforded by the more general notion of context-free grammar, consider a CFG over the category
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}^{\textrm{\^{}}\$}}
from Example
1.2
.
Such a grammar may include production rules that can only be applied upon reaching the end of the input or only at the beginning, which is useful in practice, albeit usually modelled in an ad hoc fashion.
For example, the grammar of arithmetic expressions defined by Knuth in the original paper on LR parsing
[
Knu65
, example (27)]
may be naturally described as a grammar over
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}^{\textrm{\^{}}\$}}
, which in addition to having three “classical” nonterminals
E,T,P\ThisStyle{\sqsubset}(\ast,\ast)
contains a distinguished nonterminal
S\ThisStyle{\sqsubset}(\ast,\top)
.
Knuth’s “zeroth” production rule
0:S\to E\$
is then just a unary node
0:E\to S
in
\mathbb{S}
, mapped by
p
to the operation
\varepsilon{-}\$:(\ast,\ast)\to(\ast,\top)
in
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}^{\textrm{\^{}}\$}}}
.

More significant examples of context-free languages of arrows over categories with more than one object will be given in §
3
, including context-free grammars over the runs of finite-state automata.

Remark 4
.

It is at times useful to consider a categorical CFG equivalently as a triple of a
pointed
finite species
(\mathbb{S},S)
, a
bipointed
category
(\mathcal{C},A,B)
, and a functor of pointed operads
p_{G}:(\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S},S)\to(\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}},(A,B))
.
Note that the operad of spliced arrows construction lifts to a functor

\mathcal{W}_{\bullet}:\mathrm{Cat}_{\bullet,\bullet}\to\mathrm{Oper}_{\bullet}

sending a category
\mathcal{C}
equipped with a pair of objects
A
and
B
to the operad
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
equipped with the color
(A,B)
,
and that the free / forgetful adjunction (
1.1
) likewise lifts to an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spec}_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Oper}_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{U}_{\bullet}}

between pointed species and pointed operads.
This permits another way of understanding the language of arrows generated by a grammar considered as a functor of pointed operads: since the set of constants of color
(A,B)
in
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
is in bijection with the set of arrows
A\to B
in
\mathcal{C}
, we have a natural isomorphism
\mathop{\mathrm{con}}\circ\,(\mathcal{W}_{\bullet})\cong\hom
for the evident functors
\hom:\mathrm{Cat}_{\bullet,\bullet}\to\mathrm{Set}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{con}}:\mathrm{Oper}_{\bullet}\to\mathrm{Set}
, and the language
\mathcal{L}_{G}
is precisely the image of the function
\mathop{\mathrm{con}}(p_{G}):\mathop{\mathrm{con}}(\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S},S)\to\mathop{\mathrm{con}}(\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}},(A,B))\cong\mathcal{C}(A,B)
.
2
2

2

It is also possible to take a more abstract and more general perspective on
\mathcal{L}_{G}
, as building an
initial model
of the grammar
p_{G}:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
in some target universe
q:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{B}
with sufficient fibrational structure. The classical case is recovered by taking
q
to be the subset fibration
\mathrm{Subset}\to\mathrm{Set}
. See
[
MZ25
]
.

1.4.
Properties of a context-free grammar and its associated language

Standard properties of context-free grammars (cf.
[
SS88
, Ch. 4]
), considered as categorical CFGs
G=(\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}},\mathbb{S},S,p)
, may be reformulated as properties of either the species
\mathbb{S}
, the operad
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
, or the functor
p:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\Sigma}
, with varying degrees of naturality:

•

G
is
linear
just in case
\mathbb{S}
only has nodes of arity
\leq 1
.
It is
left-linear
(respectively,
right-linear
) just in case it is linear and every unary node
x
of
\mathbb{S}
is mapped by
p
to an operation of the form
\varepsilon{-}w
(resp.
p(x)=w{-}\varepsilon
).

•

G
is in
Chomsky normal form
if
\mathbb{S}
only has nodes of arity 2 or 0, the color
S
does not appear as the input of any node, every binary node is mapped by
p
to
\varepsilon{-}\varepsilon{-}\varepsilon
in
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}}
, and every nullary node is mapped to a letter
a\in\Sigma
, unless
R=S
in which case it is possible that
p(x)=\varepsilon
.
(These conditions can be made a bit more more natural by considering
G
as a context-free grammar over
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}^{\textrm{\^{}}\$}}
with
S\ThisStyle{\sqsubset}(\ast,\top)
, see Example
1.3
above.)

•

G
is
bilinear
(a generalization of Chomsky normal form
[
LL09
,
Lee89
]
) iff
\mathbb{S}
only has nodes of arity
\leq 2
.

•

G
is
unambiguous
iff for any pair of constants
\alpha,\beta:S
in
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
, if
p(\alpha)=p(\beta)
then
\alpha=\beta
.
Note that if
p
is faithful then
G
is unambiguous, although faithfulness is a stronger condition in general.

•

A nonterminal
R
of
G
is
nullable
if there exists a constant
\alpha:R
of
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
such that
p(\alpha)=\varepsilon
.

•

A nonterminal
R
of
G
is
useful
if there exists a pair of a constant
\alpha:R
and a unary operation
\beta:R\to S
.
Note that if
G
has no useless nonterminals then
G
is unambiguous iff
p
is faithful.

Observe that almost all of these properties can be immediately translated to express properties of context-free grammars over
any
category
\mathcal{C}
. Basic closure properties
[
HMU07
, §7.3]
of classical context-free languages (CFLs) also generalize easily to CFLs of arrows.

Proposition 5
.

(1)

If
\mathcal{L}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{L}_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(A,B)
are CFLs of arrows, so is their union
\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(A,B)
.

(2)

If
\mathcal{L}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(A_{1},B_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{L}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(A_{n},B_{n})
are CFLs of arrows, and if
w_{0}{-}\dots{-}w_{n}:(A_{1},B_{1}),\dots,(A_{n},B_{n})\to(A,B)
is an operation of
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
, then the “spliced concatenation”
w_{0}\mathcal{L}_{1}w_{1}\dots\mathcal{L}_{n}w_{n}=\{\,{w_{0}u_{1}w_{1}\dots u_{n}w_{n}\mid u_{1}\in\mathcal{L}_{1},\dots,u_{n}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}}\,\}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(A,B)
is also context-free.

(3)

If
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(A,B)
is a CFL of arrows in a category
\mathcal{C}
and
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a functor of categories, then the functorial image
F(\mathcal{L})\subseteq\mathcal{D}(F(A),F(B))
is also context-free.

Proof 1.1
.

The proofs of (1) and (2) are just refinements of the standard proofs for context-free languages of words, keeping track of the underlying gap types:

(1)

Given grammars
G_{i}=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S}_{i},S_{i},p_{i})
for
i=1\dots k
, where all of the
S_{i}
refine the same gap type
(A,B)
, we define a new grammar
G=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S},S,p)
that generates the union of the languages
\mathcal{L}_{G}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}_{G_{i}}
by taking
\mathbb{S}
to be the disjoint union of the colors and nodes of
\mathbb{S}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{S}_{k}
combined with a distinguished color
S
and unary nodes
x_{i}:S_{i}\to S
for
i=1\dots k
, and defining
\phi:\mathbb{S}\to{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}}
to be the cotupling of
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{k}
extended by the mappings
S\mapsto(A,B)
and
x_{i}\mapsto id_{A}{-}id_{B}
for
i=1\dots k
.

(2)

Given grammars
G_{1}=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S}_{1},S_{1},p_{1})
, …,
G_{n}=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S}_{n},S_{n},p_{n})
where
S_{i}\ThisStyle{\sqsubset}(A_{i},B_{i})
for each
1\leq i\leq n
, together with an operation

w_{0}{-}\dots{-}w_{n}:(A_{1},B_{1}),\dots,(A_{n},B_{n})\to(A,B)
of
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
, we construct a new grammar
G=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S},S,p)
that generates the spliced concatenation
w_{0}\mathcal{L}_{G_{1}}w_{1}\dots\mathcal{L}_{G_{n}}w_{n}
by taking
\mathbb{S}
to be the disjoint union of the colors and nodes of
\mathbb{S}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{S}_{n}
combined with a distinguished color
S
and a single
n
-ary node
x:S_{1},\dots,S_{n}\to S
, and defining
\phi:\mathbb{S}\to{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}}
to be the cotupling of
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{n}
extended by the mappings
S\mapsto(A,B)
and
x\mapsto w_{0}{-}\dots{-}w_{n}
.

For (3), suppose given a grammar
G=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S},S,p)
and a functor of categories
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
.
Then the grammar
F(G)
generating the language
F(\mathcal{L}_{G})
is defined by postcomposing
p
with
\mathcal{W}\,{F}:\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{D}}
while keeping the species
\mathbb{S}
and start symbol
S
the same,
F(G)=(\mathcal{D},\mathbb{S},S,(\mathcal{W}\,{F})\circ p)
.

Finally, we note that there is a natural definition of morphisms between context-free grammars over the same category, which we refer to as
translations
.

{defi}

Let
G_{1}=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S}_{1},S_{1},p_{1})
and
G_{2}=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S}_{2},S_{2},p_{2})
be two grammars over the same category.
A
translation

T:G_{1}\to G_{2}
is given by a functor of operads
T:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}_{1}\to\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}_{2}
that commutes with the projection functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}

and preserves the start symbol
T(S_{1})=S_{2}
.
We say that the translation is
full
and/or
faithful
just in case the underlying functor
T:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}_{1}\to\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}_{2}
is full and/or faithful.

Proposition 6
.

For any translation
T:G_{1}\to G_{2}
, we have
\mathcal{L}_{G_{1}}\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{G_{2}}
.
If
T
is full then
\mathcal{L}_{G_{1}}=\mathcal{L}_{G_{2}}
, and if
T
is both full and faithful then the grammars moreover generate isomorphic sets of parse trees for every arrow of the language.

Proof 1.2
.

A translation induces a function from constants
\alpha:S_{1}
of
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}_{1}
to constants
\beta=T(\alpha):S_{2}
of
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}_{2}
lying over the same arrow
p_{1}(\alpha)=p_{2}(\beta)
, and hence
\mathcal{L}_{G_{1}}\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{G_{2}}
.
If the translation is full (respectively, fully faithful), then this function is surjective (respectively bijective), which implies that
\mathcal{L}_{G_{1}}=\mathcal{L}_{G_{2}}
, with a bijection between sets of derivations,

\{\,{\alpha:S_{1}\mid p_{1}(\alpha)=w}\,\}\cong\{\,{\beta:S_{2}\mid p_{2}(\beta)=w}\,\}

for every arrow
w
, in the case that
T
is fully faithful.

We refer to the conclusion of Proposition
6
under the strongest condition that the functor
T
is fully faithful as the
strong translation principle
.

1.5.
A fibrational view of parsing as a lifting problem

We have seen how any context-free grammar
G=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S},S,p)
gives rise to a language

\mathcal{L}_{G}=\{\,{p(\alpha)\mid\alpha:S}\,\}
, corresponding to the arrows of
\mathcal{C}
which, seen as constants of
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
, are in the image of some constant of color
S
of the free operad
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
.
However, beyond characterizing the language defined by a grammar, in practice one is often confronted with a dual problem, namely that of parsing: given a word
w
, we want to compute the set of all its parse trees, or at least determine all of the nonterminals which derive it.
In our functorial formulation of context-free derivations, this amounts to computing the inverse image of
w
along the functor
p
, i.e., the set of constants
p^{-1}(w)=\{\,{\alpha\mid p(\alpha)=w}\,\}
, or alternatively the set of colors in the image of
p^{-1}(w)
along the output-color function.

To better understand this view of parsing as a lifting problem along a functor of operads, we find it helpful to first recall the correspondence between functors of categories
p:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
and lax functors
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
, where
\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
is the bicategory whose objects are sets, whose 1-cells
S:X\,{{\,\longrightarrow\hskip-11.49995pt|\hskip 9.49997pt}}Y
are spans
X\leftarrow S\rightarrow Y
,
and whose 2-cells are morphisms of spans.
Suppose given such a functor
p:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
.
To every object
A
of
\mathcal{C}
there is an associated fiber
F(A)=p^{-1}(A)
of objects in
\mathcal{D}
living over
A
, while to every arrow
w:A\to B
of
\mathcal{C}
there is an associated fiber
F(w)=p^{-1}(w)
of arrows in
\mathcal{D}
living over
w
, equipped with a pair of projection functions
F(A)\leftarrow F(w)\rightarrow F(B)
mapping any lifting
\alpha:R\to S
of
w:A\to B
to its source
R\in F(A)
and target
S\in F(B)
.
Moreover, given a pair of composable arrows

u:A\to B
and
v:B\to C
in
\mathcal{C}
, there is a morphism of spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(u)F(v)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(uv)}\hskip 8.19447pt:\hskip 8.19447pt{F(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(C)}}
|

(1)

from the composite of the spans

F({u}):F({A}){\,\longrightarrow\hskip-11.49995pt|\hskip 9.49997pt}F({B})

and

F({v}):F({B}){\,\longrightarrow\hskip-11.49995pt|\hskip 9.49997pt}F({C})
associated
to
u:A\to B
and
v:B\to C
to the span
F({uv}):F({A}){\,\longrightarrow\hskip-11.49995pt|\hskip 9.49997pt}F({C})
associated to the composite arrow
uv:A\to C
.
This morphism of spans is realized using composition in the category
\mathcal{D}
,
namely by the function taking any pair of a lifting
\alpha:R\to S
of
u
and a lifting
\beta:S\to T
of
v

to the composite
\alpha\beta:R\to T
, which is a lifting of
uv
by functoriality
p(\alpha\beta)=p(\alpha)p(\beta)=uv
.
Similarly, the identity arrows in the category
\mathcal{D}
define, for every object
A
of the category
\mathcal{C}
,
a morphism of spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{id_{F(A)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F({id_{A}})}\hskip 8.19447pt:\hskip 8.19447pt{F({A})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F({A})}}
|

(2)

from the identity span
F(A)\leftarrow F(A)\rightarrow F(A)

to the span associated to the identity arrow
id_{A}:A\to A
.
Associativity and neutrality of composition in
\mathcal{D}

ensure that the 2-cells (
1
) and (
2
) make the diagrams below commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(u)F(v)F(w)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(u)F({vw})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F({uv})F(w)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F({uvw})}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F({u})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F({id_{A}})F({u})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F({u})}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F({u})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F({u})F({id_{B}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F({u})}}

for all triples of composable arrows
u:A\to B
,
v:B\to C
and
w:C\to D
, and therefore that
this collection of data defines what is called a lax functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
.
In general it is
only
lax, in the sense that the 2-cells
F(u)F(v)\Rightarrow F({uv})
and
id_{F(A)}\Rightarrow F({id_{A}})
are not necessarily invertible.

Conversely, starting from the data provided by a lax functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
, we can define a category denoted
\smallint_{\mathcal{C}}F
together with a functor
\pi:\smallint_{\mathcal{C}}F\to\mathcal{C}
.
The category
\smallint_{\mathcal{C}}F
has objects the pairs
(A,R)
of an object
A
in
\mathcal{C}
and an element
R\in F(A)
,
and arrows
(w,\alpha):(A,R)\to(B,S)
the pairs of an arrow
w:A\to B
in
\mathcal{C}
and an element
\alpha\in F(w)
mapped to
R\in F(A)
and
S\in F(B)
by the respective legs of the span:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(w)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

Here we slightly abuse notation by writing
F(w)
for the apex of the span
F(A){\,\longrightarrow\hskip-11.49995pt|\hskip 9.49997pt}F(B)
assigned to
w:A\to B
.
The composition and identity of the category
\smallint_{\mathcal{C}}F
are then given by the morphisms of spans
F(u)F(v)\Rightarrow F({uv})
and
id_{F(A)}\Rightarrow F({id_{A}})
witnessing the lax functoriality of
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
.
The functor
\pi:\smallint_{\mathcal{C}}F\to\mathcal{C}
is given by the first projection.
This construction of a category
\smallint_{\mathcal{C}}F
equipped with a functor
\pi:\smallint_{\mathcal{C}}F\to\mathcal{C}
starting from a lax functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
, apparently first made explicit in
[
PA97
, Prop. 4]
, is a mild variation of Bénabou’s construction
[
Bén72
,
Bén00
]
of the same starting from a lax normal functor
F:\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Dist}
, which is itself a generalization of the well-known Grothendieck construction of a fibration starting from a pseudofunctor
F:\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Cat}
.
One can show that given a functor of categories

p:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
, the construction
applied to the associated lax functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})

induces a category
\smallint_{\mathcal{C}}F
isomorphic to
\mathcal{D}
,
in such a way that
p
coincides with the isomorphism composed with
\pi
.
Recently, Ahrens and Lumsdaine
[
AL19
]
have introduced the useful terminology “displayed category” to refer to this way of presenting a category
\mathcal{D}
equipped with a functor
\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
as a lax functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
, with their motivations coming from formalization of mathematics.

The constructions which turn a functor of categories
p:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
into a lax functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
and back into a functor
\pi:\smallint_{\mathcal{C}}F\to\mathcal{C}
can be adapted smoothly to functors of operads, viewing
\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
as a 2-categorical operad whose
n
-ary operations

S:X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\,{{\,\longrightarrow\hskip-11.49995pt|\hskip 9.49997pt}}Y
are multi-legged-spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}}

or equivalently spans
X_{1}\times\dots\times X_{n}\leftarrow S\rightarrow Y
,
and with the same notion of 2-cell.
We will follow Ahrens and Lumsdaine’s suggestion and refer to the data of such a lax functor
F:\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
representing an operad
\mathcal{D}\cong\smallint_{\mathcal{O}}F
equipped with a functor
p:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{O}
as a
displayed operad
.

1.6.
An inductive formula and a sequent calculus for displayed free operads

It is folklore that the free operad over a species
\mathbb{S}=(C,V,i,o)
may be described concretely as a certain family of trees: operations of
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
are interpreted as (rooted planar) trees whose edges are colored by the elements of
C
and whose nodes are labelled by the elements of
V
, subject to the constraints imposed by the functions
i:V\to C^{*}
and
o:V\to C
.
The formal construction of the free operad may be viewed as a free monoid construction, adapted to a situation where the ambient monoidal product (in this case, the composition product of species) is only distributive on the left, see
[
MSS02
, II.1.9]
and
[
BJT97
, Appendix B]
.

From the perspective of programming semantics, it is natural to consider the underlying species of
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
as an inductive data type, corresponding to the initial algebra for the endofunctor
W_{\mathbb{S}}
on
C
-colored species defined by

W_{\mathbb{S}}=\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{I}+\mathbb{S}\circ\mathbb{R}

where
+
denotes the coproduct of
C
-colored species which is constructed by taking the disjoint union of nodes, while
\circ
and
\mathbb{I}
denote respectively the composition product of
C
-colored species and the identity species, defined as follows.
Given two
C
-colored species
\mathbb{S}
and
\mathbb{R}
,
the
n
-ary nodes
R_{1},\dots,R_{n}\to S

of
\mathbb{S}\circ\mathbb{R}

are formal composites
g\bullet(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
consisting of a node

g:S_{1},\dots,S_{k}\to S
of
\mathbb{S}
and of a tuple of nodes

f_{1}:\Gamma_{1}\to S_{1}
,
\dots
,
f_{k}:\Gamma_{k}\to S_{k}
of
\mathbb{R}
,
such that the concatenation of the lists of colors
\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{k}
is equal to the list
R_{1},\dots,R_{n}
.
The unit
\mathbb{I}
is the
C
-colored species with a single unary node
\ast_{R}:R\to R
for every color
R\in C
,
and no other nodes.

As the initial
W_{\mathbb{S}}
-algebra, the free operad over
\mathbb{S}
is equipped with a map of species

\mathbb{I}+\mathbb{S}\circ\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
,
which by the Lambek Lemma is invertible,
with the following interpretation: any operation of
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
is either an identity operation, or the parallel composition of a node of
\mathbb{S}
with a list of operations of
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
.
Note that this interpretation also corresponds to a canonical decomposition

(3)

of rooted planar trees, by iterated examination of the root.
An operation of
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
may be seen as a rooted planar tree whose edges and nodes are labelled by the species
\mathbb{S}
, and we sometimes refer to such an operation as an
\mathbb{S}
-rooted tree
(cf.
[
BLL98
, §3.2]
).

It is possible to derive an analogous inductive characterization of
functors

p:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{O}
from a free operad into an arbitrary operad
\mathcal{O}
considered as displayed free operads, i.e., as lax functors
F:\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
generated by an underlying map of species
\phi:\mathbb{S}\to{\mathcal{O}}
.
Two subtleties arise.
First, that the species
\mathbb{S}
and the operad
\mathcal{O}
may in general have a different set of colors, related by the change-of-color function
\phi_{C}
.
To account for this, rather than restricting the operations
+,\circ,\mathbb{I}
to the category of
C
-colored species,
one should consider them as global functors

+,\circ:\mathrm{Spec}\times_{\mathrm{Set}}\mathrm{Spec}\to\mathrm{Spec}\qquad\mathbb{I}:\mathrm{Set}\to\mathrm{Spec}

on the “polychromatic” category of species, which respect the underlying sets of colors in a functorial way.
Second, and more significantly, the above functor
W_{\mathbb{S}}
transports a species
\mathbb{R}
living over
{\mathcal{O}}
to a species living over
\mathbb{I}+{\mathcal{O}}\circ{\mathcal{O}}
, so that in order to obtain again a species living over
{\mathcal{O}}
(and thus define an endofunctor) one needs to “push forward” along the canonical
W_{{\mathcal{O}}}
-algebra
[e,m]:\mathbb{I}+{\mathcal{O}}\circ{\mathcal{O}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}
that encodes the operad structure of
\mathcal{O}
, seen as a monoid in
(\mathrm{Spec},\circ,\mathbb{I})
.
A detailed discussion is beyond the scope of this paper, but we nevertheless state the following proposition, whose intuitive content should be clear:

Proposition 7
.

Let
\phi:\mathbb{S}\to{\mathcal{O}}
be a map of species from a species
\mathbb{S}
into an operad
\mathcal{O}
, and let
p:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{O}
be the corresponding functor from the free operad.
Then the associated lax functor
F:\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
computing the fibers of
p
is given by
F(A)=\phi^{-1}(A)
on colors of
\mathcal{O}
, and by the least family of spans
F(f)
indexed by operations
f:A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\to A
of
\mathcal{O}
such that

F(f)\cong\sum_{{f=id_{A}}\atop{\phi(R)=A}}id_{R}\ \ +\sum_{f=g\circ(h_{1},\dots,h_{k})}\phi^{-1}(g)\bullet(F({h_{1}}),\dots,F({h_{k}}))

(4)

where we write
\circ
for composition in the operad
\mathcal{O}
and
\bullet
for formal composition of nodes in
\mathbb{S}
with operations in
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
.
Specializing the formula to constant operations, the left summand disappears and (
4
) simplifies to:

F(c)\cong\sum_{c=g\circ(c_{1},\dots,c_{k})}\phi^{-1}(g)\bullet(F({c_{1}}),\dots,F({c_{k}}))

(5)

For the benefit of readers with a background in logic, we note that the inductive characterization of displayed free operads may be equivalently expressed as a sequent calculus parameterized by an underlying map of species
\phi:\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{O}
, see Figure
3
.
Adapting a convention from our type refinement systems work, we write

R_{1},\dots,R_{k}\ThisStyle{\Rightarrow_{g}^{\phi}}R

for the judgment whose evidence is provided by a node
x:R_{1},\dots,R_{k}\to R
in
\mathbb{S}
such that
\phi(x)=g
, and similarly

S_{1},\dots,S_{n}\ThisStyle{\Rightarrow_{f}^{p}}R

for the judgment whose evidence is provided by an operation
f:S_{1},\dots,S_{n}\to R
in
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
such that
p(\alpha)=f
.
Again, we write
R\ThisStyle{\sqsubset^{p}}A
to indicate that
R
is a color of
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
with image
p(R)=A
in
\mathcal{O}
.
As standard, we treat the inference rules as an inductive definition, in the sense that the set of derivations of the calculus is the least set closed under the inference rules.
Then formulas (
4
) and (
5
) are equivalent to the following:

Proposition 8
.

For the sequent calculus of Fig.
3
, parameterized with respect to a map of species
\phi:\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{O}
and the corresponding functor
p:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{O}
, derivations of
\Gamma\ThisStyle{\Rightarrow_{f}^{p}}R
are in one-to-one correspondence with operations
\alpha:\Gamma\to R
in
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
such that
p(\alpha)=f
.
As a special case, constants
\alpha:R
such that
p(\alpha)=c
are in one-to-one correspondence with derivations of
\ThisStyle{\Rightarrow_{c}^{p}}R
, which necessarily only use instances of the
node
rule.

\frac{R\ThisStyle{\sqsubset^{p}}A}{R\ThisStyle{\Rightarrow_{id_{A}}^{p}}R}\quad\textsc{vcenter,Left={leaf}}\qquad\qquad\frac{f=g\circ(h_{1},\dots,h_{k})\quad\quad R_{1},\dots,R_{k}\ThisStyle{\Rightarrow_{g}^{\phi}}R\quad\Gamma_{1}\ThisStyle{\Rightarrow_{h_{1}}^{p}}R_{1}\quad\dots\quad\Gamma_{k}\ThisStyle{\Rightarrow_{h_{k}}^{p}}R_{k}}{\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{k}\ThisStyle{\Rightarrow_{f}^{p}}R}\quad\textsc{vcenter,Left={node}}

Figure 3.
A sequent calculus for displayed free operads.

1.7.
Application to parsing

Instantiating (
5
) with the underlying functor of a categorical CFG generated by a map of species
\phi:\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
, we immediately obtain the following characteristic formula for the family of sets of parse trees
F(w)
of an arrow
w
in
\mathcal{C}
, seen as liftings of the constant
w
in
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
to a constant in
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
:

F(w)\cong\sum_{w=w_{0}u_{1}w_{1}\dots u_{n}w_{k}}\phi^{-1}(w_{0}{-}\dots{-}w_{k})\bullet(F({u_{1}}),\dots,F({u_{k}}))

(6)

Equivalently, specializing the sequent calculus of Figure
3
and translating the generic judgment forms into traditional CFG notation by

\displaystyle R_{1},\dots,R_{k}\ThisStyle{\Rightarrow_{w_{0}{-}\dots{-}w_{n}}^{\phi}}R

\displaystyle\quad\leadsto\quad R\to w_{0}R_{1}w_{1}\dots R_{n}w_{n}

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\Rightarrow_{w}^{p}}R

\displaystyle\quad\leadsto\quad R\to^{+}w

we obtain the following inference rule:

\frac{w=w_{0}u_{1}w_{1}\dots u_{k}w_{k}\quad R\to w_{0}R_{1}w_{1}\dots R_{k}w_{k}\quad R_{1}\to^{+}u_{1}\quad\dots\quad R_{k}\to^{+}u_{k}}{R\to^{+}w}

(7)

inductively defining the set of closed context-free derivations.
For any given word
w
, let
N_{w}=\{\,{R\mid R\to^{+}w}\,\}
be the set of nonterminals that derive it, corresponding to the image of
F(w)
along the function returning the root label of a parse tree.
We have that:

\mathtoolsset{multlined-width=0.9}\multlined R\in N_{w}\iff w=w_{0}u_{1}w_{1}\dots u_{k}w_{k}\ \wedge\\
R\to w_{0}R_{1}w_{1}\dots R_{k}w_{k}\wedge R_{1}\in N_{u_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge R_{k}\in N_{u_{k}}

(8)

This equation is essentially the characteristic formula expressed by Leermakers
[
Lee89
]
for the defining relation of the “C-parser”, which generalizes the well-known Cocke-Younger-Kasami (CYK) algorithm.
Presentations of the CYK algorithm are usually restricted to grammars in Chomsky normal form (cf.
[
LL09
]
), but as observed by Leermakers, the relation
N_{w}
defined by (
8
) can be solved effectively for any context-free grammar
G
and given word
w=a_{1}\dots a_{n}
by building up a parse matrix
N_{i,j}
indexed by the subwords
w_{i,j}=a_{i+1}\dots a_{j}
for all
1\leq i\leq j\leq n
, and yields a cubic complexity algorithm in the case that
G
is bilinear in the sense of §
1.4
.
Moreover, by adding nonterminals, it is always possible to transform a CFG into a bilinear CFG that generates the same language, even preserving the original derivations up to isomorphism.

Proposition 9
.

For any categorical context-free grammar
G=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S},S,p)
, there is a bilinear CFG
G_{\mathsf{bin}}=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S}_{\mathsf{bin}},S,p_{\mathsf{bin}})
together with a fully faithful functor of operads

B:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}_{\mathsf{bin}}
such that
p=Bp_{\mathsf{bin}}
.
In particular, by the strong translation principle (Prop.
6
) we have that
\mathcal{L}_{G}=\mathcal{L}_{G_{\mathsf{bin}}}
with isomorphic sets of parse trees.

Proof 1.3
.

Given
G=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S},S,p)
, a bilinear grammar
G_{\mathsf{bin}}=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S}_{\mathsf{bin}},S,p_{\mathsf{bin}})
is constructed as follows.

\mathbb{S}_{\mathsf{bin}}
includes all of the colors and all of the nullary nodes of
\mathbb{S}
, with
\phi_{\mathsf{bin}}(R)=\phi(R)
and
\phi_{\mathsf{bin}}(c)=\phi(c)
.
Additionally, for every node
x:R_{1},\dots,R_{n}\to R
of
\mathbb{S}
of positive arity
n>0
, where
\phi(x)=w_{0}{-}\dots{-}w_{n}:(A_{1},B_{1}),\dots,(A_{n},B_{n})\to(A,B)
in
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
, we include:

•

n
new colors
I_{x,0},\dots,I_{x,n-1}
, with
\phi_{\mathsf{bin}}(I_{x,i-1})=(A,A_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq n
;

•

one nullary node
x_{0}:I_{x,0}
, with
\phi_{\mathsf{bin}}(x_{0})=w_{0}
;

•

n
binary nodes
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
, where
x_{i}:I_{x,i-1},R_{i}\to I_{x,i}
and
\phi_{\mathsf{bin}}(x_{i})=id_{A}{-}id_{A_{i}}{-}w_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
, under the convention that
I_{x,n}=R
.

We define the functor
B:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}_{\mathsf{bin}}
on colors by
B(R)=R
, on nullary nodes by
B(c)=c
, and on nodes
x:R_{1},\dots,R_{n}\to R
of positive arity by

B(x)=x_{n}\circ_{0}\dots\circ_{0}x_{1}\circ_{0}x_{0}
.
By induction on
n
, there is a one-to-one correspondence between nodes
x:R_{1},\dots,R_{n}\to R
of
\mathbb{S}
and operations
B(x):R_{1},\dots,R_{n}\to R
of
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}_{\mathsf{bin}}
, so the functor
B
is fully faithful.

1.8.
Generalized context-free grammars

We conclude this section by observing that for many purposes, a functor of operads from a free operad generated by a finite species
\mathbb{S}
into an arbitrary operad
\mathcal{O}
may be considered (after selection of a start symbol in
\mathbb{S}
) as a “generalized” context-free grammar, generating a language of constants in
\mathcal{O}
.

{defi}

A
generalized context-free grammar (over an operad)
is a tuple
G=(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{S},S,p)
consisting of an operad
\mathcal{O}
, a finite species
\mathbb{S}
equipped with a distinguished color
S\in\mathbb{S}
, and a functor of operads
p:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{O}
.
The
generalized context-free language of constants
generated by the grammar
G
is given by the subset of constants
\mathcal{L}_{G}=\{\,{p(\alpha)\mid\alpha:S}\,\}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(A)
, where
p(S)=A
.

Hence a classical context-free word grammar can be seen as a generalized CFG (gCFG) over a spliced word operad
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{W}\,{\Sigma}
, and a categorical CFG as a gCFG over a spliced arrow operad
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
.
By selecting
\mathcal{O}
appropriately, Definition
1.8
encompasses other extensions of the notion of context-free grammar from the literature.

{exa}

For any operad
\mathcal{P}
, one can construct a new operad
!_{\mathsf{aff}}{\mathcal{P}}
whose colors are lists
[A_{1},\dots,A_{k}]
of colors in
\mathcal{P}
and whose
n
-ary operations
[\Gamma_{1}],\dots,[\Gamma_{n}]\to[A_{1},\dots,A_{k}]
are pairs
([f_{1},\dots,f_{k}],\sigma)
of a list of operations

f_{1}:\Omega_{1}\to A_{1},\dots,f_{k}:\Omega_{k}\to A_{k}

in
\mathcal{P}
and an injection
\sigma:\Omega_{1},\dots,\Omega_{k}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{n}
from the concatenation of the inputs of the
k
operations to the concatenation of the
n
input lists.
Observe that if
\mathcal{P}
is an uncolored operad (i.e., has a unique color, like
\mathcal{W}\,{\Sigma}
), then the colors of
!_{\mathsf{aff}}{\mathcal{P}}
are isomorphic to natural numbers.
Let us also remark that
!_{\mathsf{aff}}{\mathcal{P}}
is in fact the
free semi-cartesian (or “affine”) strict monoidal operad
over
\mathcal{P}
.
3
3

3

See
[
Lei04
, p. 56–58]
for discussion of the similar constructions of the free PRO(P) over a colored operad.

A gCFG over
!_{\mathsf{aff}}{\mathcal{W}\,{\Sigma}}
with start symbol
S\ThisStyle{\sqsubset}1
is precisely a
multiple context-free grammar

[
SMFK91
]
, while more generally a gCFG over
!_{\mathsf{aff}}{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}}
with
S\ThisStyle{\sqsubset}[(A,B)]
(for some objects
A
and
B
of
\mathcal{C}
) could be called a “multiple categorical CFG”.
Such a grammar is a
k
-multiple CFG
(again in the sense of
[
SMFK91
]
) just in case every nonterminal
R\ThisStyle{\sqsubset}[\Gamma]
refines a list of length
|\Gamma|\leq k
.

For instance, let
\mathcal{C}
be the free category over the following graph:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\#}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\#^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

We can define a 3-mCFG generating the language
a^{n}\#b^{n}\#^{\prime}c^{n}
with a pair of nonterminals

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\sqsubset}[(A,C)]

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\sqsubset}[(A,A),(B,B),(C,C)]

and production rules specified by the triple of nodes in
\mathbb{S}

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle:R

\displaystyle x_{2}

\displaystyle:R\to R

\displaystyle x_{3}

\displaystyle:R\to S

mapped respectively to the following operations in
!_{\mathsf{aff}}{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}}

\displaystyle[id_{A},id_{B},id_{C}]

\displaystyle:[(A,A),(B,B),(C,C)]

\displaystyle[a{-}id_{A},b{-}id_{B},c{-}id_{C}]

\displaystyle:[(A,A),(B,B),(C,C)]\to[(A,A),(B,B),(C,C)]

\displaystyle[{-}\#{-}\#^{\prime}{-}]

\displaystyle:[(A,A),(B,B),(C,C)]\to[(A,C)]

where we have elided the injection component and only shown the lists of operations in
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
, since the injection
\sigma
used is always the identity.

{exa}

The
free cartesian strict monoidal operad

!_{\mathsf{cart}}{\mathcal{P}}
over an operad
\mathcal{P}
is constructed just as
!_{\mathsf{aff}}{\mathcal{P}}
, but replacing injections by arbitrary functions
\sigma:\Omega_{1},\dots,\Omega_{k}\to\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{n}
.
A gCFG over
!_{\mathsf{cart}}{\mathcal{W}\,{\Sigma}}
is precisely a
parallel multiple context-free grammar
in the sense of
[
SMFK91
]
, and more generally a gCFG over
!_{\mathsf{cart}}{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}}
could be called a “parallel multiple categorical CFG”.

Examples of generalized context-free languages of a more semantic flavor can also be derived by taking
\mathcal{O}
to be
\mathrm{Set}
or similar.
Recall that
\mathrm{Set}
is the (large) operad whose colors are sets and whose operations
f:X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\to X
are
n
-ary functions
f:X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{n}\to X
, with composition defined as expected.

{exa}

(Adapted from
[
CE12
, §1.1.3]
.)
Let
\mathrm{DiGr}_{\bullet,\bullet}
be the set of finite directed graphs equipped with two distinct vertices labeled
\mathsf{src}
and
\mathsf{tgt}
.
The
parallel composition

G\parallel H
of a pair of graphs
(G,H)\in\mathrm{DiGr}_{\bullet,\bullet}\times\mathrm{DiGr}_{\bullet,\bullet}
is formed by starting from the disjoint union of the two graphs and identifying their two sources and two targets to form a graph
G\parallel H\in\mathrm{DiGr}_{\bullet,\bullet}
with
\mathsf{src}(G\parallel H)=\mathsf{src}(G)=\mathsf{src}(H)
and
\mathsf{tgt}(G\parallel H)=\mathsf{tgt}(G)=\mathsf{tgt}(H)
.
The
series composition

G\mathbin{;}H
is formed by similarly starting from the disjoint union of
G
and
H
but then identifying
\mathsf{tgt}(G)=\mathsf{src}(H)
and taking
\mathsf{src}(G\mathbin{;}H)=\mathsf{src}(G)
and
\mathsf{tgt}(G\mathbin{;}H)=\mathsf{tgt}(H)
.
We can define a gCFG over
\mathrm{Set}
with one nonterminal
S\ThisStyle{\sqsubset}\mathrm{DiGr}_{\bullet,\bullet}
and three production rules corresponding to a pair of binary nodes
par:S,S\to S
and
seq:S,S\to S
mapped to the operations
\parallel
and
\mathbin{;}
respectively, plus a constant
e:S
mapped to the unique directed graph
\bullet\to\bullet
with one edge and two vertices.
The language of constants generated by this gCFG is exactly the set of
series-parallel graphs
.

Many of the properties of categorical CFGs discussed in §
1.4
extend directly to gCFGs, as do all of the closure properties of CFLs and the translation principles.
For the record, we state here the appropriate generalizations for gCFLs of the closure properties listed in Proposition
5
, omitting the proofs since they are essentially identical.

Proposition 10
.

(1)

If
\mathcal{L}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{L}_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(A)
are gCFLs, then so is their union
\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(A)
.

(2)

If
\mathcal{L}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(A_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{L}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(A_{n})
are gCFLs, and if
f:A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\to A
is an operation of
\mathcal{O}
, then the application
f(\mathcal{L}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{L}_{n})=\{\,{f(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})\mid u_{1}\in\mathcal{L}_{1},\dots,u_{n}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}}\,\}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(A)
is also a gCFL.

(3)

If
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(A)
is a gCFL of constants in an operad
\mathcal{O}
and
F:\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{P}
is a functor of operads, then the functorial image
F(L)\subseteq\mathcal{P}(F(A))
is also a gCFL.

1.9.
Related work

Although the notion of context-free grammar over a category seems to be new, the idea of viewing classical context-free grammars fibrationally and representing them as functors appears in various guises in the literature.
Walters briefly described in
[
Wal89
]
how context-free grammars may be represented by certain morphisms of multigraphs that we would call maps of species (see comment immediately after Definition
1.1
) and might notate
\phi:\mathbb{S}\to\tilde{\Sigma}
, where
\tilde{\Sigma}
is defined similarly to the underlying species of
\mathcal{W}\,{\Sigma}
but with two distinct kinds of nodes: nullary nodes
a:*
for every letter
a\in\Sigma
, and
n
-ary nodes
\mu_{n}:*^{n}\to*
standing for concatenation.
Note that taking
\tilde{\Sigma}
so-defined as the target of
\phi
imposes a restriction on the grammar (similar to Chomsky normal form), that all production rules are either of the form
R\to a
or
R\to R_{1}\dots R_{n}
.
In order to recover the language generated by a grammar with start symbol
S
, Walters next considers the functor
\mathcal{F}_{\times}\phi:\mathcal{F}_{\times}\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{F}_{\times}\tilde{\Sigma}
between the
free categories with products
generated by the species, then composes with the canonical functor
\psi:\mathcal{F}_{\times}\tilde{\Sigma}\to\textrm{Mon}_{\Sigma}
into the Lawvere theory of monoids containing
\Sigma
, and finally takes the image
\psi(\mathcal{F}_{\times}\phi(\mathcal{F}_{\times}\mathbb{S}(1,S)))
.
All in all, we find the encoding of classical CFGs by functors of operads
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\Sigma}
to be simpler, more direct and more general, but Walters’ approach is certainly similar in spirit.

The methodology of
abstract categorial grammars
introduced by De Groote
[
dG01
]
is probably even more closely related to our approach.
Based on linear lambda-calculus, the approach consists of expressing different kinds of grammars by specifying a signature
\Sigma_{1}
as an “abstract vocabulary”, a signature
\Sigma_{2}
as an “object vocabulary”, and a “lexicon” given by a morphism of signatures
\Sigma_{1}\to\Sigma_{2}
.
In particular, context-free grammars are represented by taking the abstract vocabulary
\Sigma_{1}
to have a type for each nonterminal and a constant
x:R_{1}\multimap\dots\multimap R_{n}\multimap R
for every production rule
x:R\to w_{0}R_{1}\dots R_{n}w_{n}
, the object vocabulary
\Sigma_{2}
to have a single type
*
and a constant
a:*\multimap*
for every letter, and the lexicon to map every nonterminal
R
to
*\multimap*
, and every constant
x
as above to the lambda-term
\lambda u_{1}\dots u_{n}.w_{0}\circ u_{1}\circ\dots\circ u_{n}\circ w_{n}
.
Although the definition of ACGs was originally given type-theoretically rather than categorically, the framework could very clearly be reformulated naturally in an operadic setting, using free closed operads, and seems to have deep connections with the approach described here.
One way of making this connection more precise could be to prove that the interpretation of pairs
(A,B)
as types
A\multimap B
extends to a full and faithful embedding
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathcal{F}_{\multimap}\,\mathcal{C}
of the operad of spliced arrows
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
into the free closed operad over
\mathcal{C}
, where the operations of
\mathcal{F}_{\multimap}\,\mathcal{C}
may be represented by
\beta\eta
-normal (ordered) linear lambda terms.
It is worth emphasizing that ACGs were introduced to capture a wide variety of grammatical formalisms, including categorial grammars in the style of Lambek calculus but also extensions of context-free grammars like we discussed in §
1.8
such as multiple CFGs
[
dGP04
]
.

2.
Finite-state automata over categories and operads

We have seen how the classical notion of context-free grammar generalizes naturally to define context-free grammars over any category
\mathcal{C}
, or even over any operad
\mathcal{O}
, presented by a functor of operads
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
or
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{O}
together with a distinguished start symbol in
\mathbb{S}
.
In this section, we carry on in the same fibrational vein and begin by explaining how classical nondeterministic finite-state word automata may be generalized to define NFAs over any category, presented by functors of categories
\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{C}
that are both
finitary
and have the
unique lifting of factorizations
(ULF) property, together with distinguished initial and accepting states in
\mathcal{Q}
.
The base category
\mathcal{C}
is often freely generated, as in the case of word automata, but we also give natural examples of automata over non-free categories.
One important aspect of this approach is that it adapts smoothly when one shifts from word automata to tree automata, simply by replacing categories with operads.
Indeed, a key fact is that the splicing construction transports any finitary ULF functor of categories
p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{C}
to a finitary ULF functor of operads
\mathcal{W}\,{p}:\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{Q}}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
, thereby transforming an NFA over a category into an NFA over its operad of spliced arrows.
As we will see in the following section (§
3
), this observation greatly facilitates the proof
that context-free languages are closed under intersection with regular languages.

2.1.
Nondeterministic word automata as finitary ULF functors over categories

Classically, a nondeterministic finite-state automaton may be represented by a finite alphabet
\Sigma
, a finite set
Q
of states, a finite set
\mathit{Tran}
of transitions together with a labelling function
\delta:\mathit{Tran}\to Q\times\Sigma\times Q
, and a choice of initial and accepting states.
We will focus first on the underlying “bare” automaton
M=(\Sigma,Q,\delta)
before the choice of initial and accepting states.
Every such bare automaton
M
induces a functor of categories
p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}

where
\mathcal{Q}
is the category with set of objects
Q
and with arrows freely generated
by arrows of the form
t:q\to q^{\prime}
for every transition
t\in\mathit{Tran}
such that
\delta(t)=(q,a,q^{\prime})
,
and where the functor
p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
transports every such generator
t:q\to q^{\prime}
to the arrow
a:\ast\to\ast
representing the letter
a\in\Sigma
in the category
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
.
See Figure
4
for an example.

Figure 4.
Left: an NFA represented with a traditional state-diagram. Right: the bare NFA as a finitary ULF functor
p:\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{Q}}\to\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
. (We do not label the generators of
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{Q}}
or indicate composite arrows, and we use colors to indicate the images of the generators in
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
.)

Under this formulation,
any composite arrow
\alpha:q_{0}\to q_{f}
of the category
\mathcal{Q}
describes
a run of the automaton
M
over the word
w=p(\alpha):\ast\to\ast
which starts in state
q_{0}\in Q
and ends in state
q_{f}\in Q
,
as depicted below:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{q_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{q_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}

One distinctive property of the functor
p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
is that it has the
unique lifting of factorizations
(ULF) property
[
Law86
,
BN00
,
BF00
,
LM10
]
.
Recall that a functor of categories has the ULF property (or “is ULF”) when any factorization of the image of an arrow lifts uniquely to a factorization of that arrow.

{defi}

A functor
p:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
is said to be
ULF
if for every arrow
\alpha
of
\mathcal{D}
, if
p(\alpha)=uv
for some pair of arrows
u
and
v
of
\mathcal{C}
,
there exists a unique pair of arrows
\beta
and
\gamma
in
\mathcal{D}
such that
\alpha=\beta\gamma
and
p(\beta)=u
and
p(\gamma)=v
.

Such functors are also known as
discrete Conduché fibrations

[
Joh99
,
Gue20
]
, and they provide a common generalization of both discrete fibrations and discrete opfibrations.
The ULF condition arises naturally when considering the correspondence recalled in §
1.5
between functors
\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
and lax functors
\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
.

Proposition 11
.

A functor of categories
p:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
is ULF iff the associated lax functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
is a pseudofunctor.

Proof 2.1
.

The definition of ULF says precisely that the structure maps
F(u)F(v)\Longrightarrow F({uv})
of the lax fiber functor are invertible, thus establishing the right-to-left direction.
Moreover, it is an immediate consequence of the ULF property that
p(\alpha)=id_{A}
implies
\alpha
is an identity arrow, since otherwise there would be two distinct liftings
\alpha=id\,\alpha
and
\alpha=\alpha\,id
of the factorization
id_{A}=id_{A}\,id_{A}
.
Hence the structure maps
id_{F(A)}\Longrightarrow F({id_{A}})
are also invertible and
F
is a pseudofunctor, thus establishing the left-to-right implication.

The ULF property reflects an important structural property of nondeterministic finite-state automata:
that every arrow
\alpha:q_{0}\to q_{f}
lying above some arrow
p(\alpha)=w
corresponding to a run of the automaton
can be factored uniquely as a sequence of transitions along the letters of the word
w
.
In the case of the ULF functor
p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
that we construct from a classical NFA,
both the categories
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
are freely generated, and
the functor
p
is generated from a homomorphism of graphs.
(In effect, on the right-hand side of Figure
4
we visualize the functor by this generating graph homomorphism.)
This is not a coincidence, as
any
ULF functor into a free category is necessarily of this form.

Proposition 12

(
[
Str96
]
,
[
Gue20
]
)
.

Let
p:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
be a functor into a category
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{G}}
freely generated by some graph
\mathbb{G}
.
Then
p
is ULF iff
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{H}}
is free over some graph
\mathbb{H}
and
p=\mathcal{F}\,{\phi}
is generated by a graph homomorphism
\phi:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{G}
.

Proof 2.2
.

The right-to-left direction is immediate. For the left-to-right implication, we take
\mathbb{H}=p^{-1}(\mathbb{G})
to be the graph containing just those arrows of
\mathcal{D}
lying over the generators of
\mathcal{C}
.
Since the image of any arrow
\alpha
in
\mathcal{D}
uniquely decomposes as a composition of edges
p(\alpha)=e_{1}\cdots e_{n}
in
\mathbb{G}
, the ULF property implies that
\alpha
uniquely decomposes as a composition of edges in
\mathbb{H}
, and
p
is generated by its restriction to the generators
\phi:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{G}
.

Of course another important property of NFAs is their finite nature, which may also be expressed as a property of the corresponding functor
p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
.

{defi}

We say that a functor
p:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
is
finitary
if either of the following equivalent conditions hold:

•

the fiber
p^{-1}(A)
as well as the fiber
p^{-1}(w)
is finite for every object
A
and arrow
w
in the category
\mathcal{C}
;

•

the associated lax functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
factors via
\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{FinSet})
.

The unique lifting of factorizations and finitary fiber properties characterize classical NFAs in the following sense.

Proposition 13
.

Let
\phi:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{G}
be a homomorphism into a finite graph
\mathbb{G}
. Then
\mathcal{F}\,{\phi}:\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{H}}\to\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{G}}
is finitary iff
\mathbb{H}
is finite.

Proof 2.3
.

By the infinite pigeonhole principle.

Proposition 14
.

A functor
p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
corresponds to the underlying bare automaton of a NFA iff
p
is ULF and finitary.

Proof 2.4
.

By Props.
12
and
13
,
p
is ULF and finitary iff
\mathcal{Q}\cong\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{Q}}
is generated by a finite graph with
p
induced by a homomorphism, thereby defining the transition graph of a bare NFA.

This leads us to the following definition of NFA over an arbitrary category.

{defi}

A
nondeterministic finite-state automaton over a category
is given by a tuple

M=(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Q},p,q_{0},q_{f})

consisting of two categories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{Q}
, a finitary ULF functor
p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{C}
,
and a pair
q_{0},q_{f}
of objects of
\mathcal{Q}
.
An object of
\mathcal{Q}
is then called a
state
and an arrow of
\mathcal{Q}
is called a
run
of the automaton.
The
regular language of arrows

\mathcal{L}_{M}
recognized by the automaton
is the set of arrows
w
in
\mathcal{C}
that can be lifted along
p
to an arrow
\alpha:q_{0}\to q_{f}
in
\mathcal{Q}
, that is
\mathcal{L}_{M}=\{\,{p(\alpha)\mid\alpha:q_{0}\to q_{f}}\,\}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(A,B)
, where
p(q_{0})=A,p(q_{f})=B
.

Similarly to the way a categorical CFG may be considered as a functor of pointed operads (see Remark
4
), it is also sometimes helpful to view a categorical NFA as a finitary ULF functor of bipointed categories
p_{M}:(\mathcal{Q},q_{0},q_{f})\to(\mathcal{C},A,B)
.
We emphasize that the definition does not require the base category
\mathcal{C}
to be free, which we will see in examples later on.

Before continuing to develop this definition, let us briefly comment on our treatment of initial and accepting states, as well as the treatment of determinism and
\epsilon
-transitions.
Rabin and Scott’s original definition of NFA
[
RS59
]
(also adopted by Eilenberg
[
Eil74
]
), takes a set of initial states together with a set of accepting states, while some more recent authors
[
HMU07
,
Sip13
]
take a single initial state together with a set of accepting states.
By Rabin and Scott’s determinization theorem, a language
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
may be recognized by a NFA with a set of initial and accepting states just in case it may be recognized by a DFA with a single initial state and a set of accepting states, so the choice of having a single initial state or a set of initial states does not matter in terms of recognition power.
Classically, the same is not quite true for NFAs over a fixed alphabet with a single initial state and a single accepting state (in the absence of
\epsilon
-transitions), since the language recognized by such an automaton is necessarily closed under concatenation if it contains the empty word.
Nevertheless, it is easy to see that any NFA with a set of initial and accepting states may be converted to an equivalent NFA with a single initial state and a single accepting state, over an alphabet extended with distinguished symbols marking the beginning and the end of the word.
This construction is particularly natural to express using the category
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}^{\textrm{\^{}}\$}}
introduced in Example
1.2
.

Proposition 15
.

A language
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
is regular in the classical sense if and only if
\textrm{\^{}}\mathcal{L}\$
is the language of arrows
recognized by a nondeterministic finite-state automaton over
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}^{\textrm{\^{}}\$}}
.

Proof 2.5
.

Given a classical NFA
M
with underlying bare automaton
\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
, we construct an NFA
M^{\textrm{\^{}}\$}=(\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}^{\textrm{\^{}}\$}},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime},p^{\prime}:\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}^{\textrm{\^{}}\$}},q_{0},q_{f})
where
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
and the functor
p^{\prime}
are constructed from
\mathcal{Q}
and
p
by freely adjoining a pair of objects
q_{0}
and
q_{f}
lying over
\bot
and
\top
respectively, together with an arrow
q_{0}\to q
over
\textrm{\^{}}:\bot\to*
for every initial state
q
and an arrow
q^{\prime}\to q_{f}
over
\$:*\to\top
for every accepting state
q^{\prime}
of
M
.
By construction, every accepting run of
M
over the word
w
corresponds to an accepting run of
M^{\textrm{\^{}}\$}
over the arrow
\textrm{\^{}}w\$:\bot\to\top
.

Although Definition
2.1
could be adapted to take a set of initial and accepting states, we find the convention of taking a single pair
(q_{0},q_{f})
of initial and accepting states to align well with the definition of context-free languages of arrows generated by a single nonterminal (Definition
1.3
). Let us also observe here that the category
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}^{\textrm{\^{}}\$}}
in the statement of Proposition
15
is isomorphic to the
input category

\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{word}}
used in Colcombet and Petrişan’s definition of categorical automata. (See bottom of Figure 1 of
[
CP20
]
; we will give a more detailed comparison with Colcombet and Petrişan’s framework in §
2.5
below.)

{defi}

An NFA
M=(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Q},{p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{C}},q_{0},q_{f})
is
deterministic
if the functor
p
is a
discrete opfibration
, i.e., for any arrow
w:A\to B
in
\mathcal{C}
and any state
q\in\mathcal{Q}
such that
p(q)=A
, there is a unique arrow
\alpha:q\to q^{\prime}
such that
p(\alpha)=w
.
M
is
codeterministic
if
p
is a
discrete fibration
, i.e., for any arrow
w:A\to B
in
\mathcal{C}
and any state
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}
such that
p(q^{\prime})=B
, there exists a unique arrow
\alpha:q\to q^{\prime}
such that
p(\alpha)=w
.
M
is
bideterministic
if it is both deterministic and codeterministic, i.e.,
p
is a discrete bifibration.
Finally,
M
is a
partial
(co/bi)deterministic automaton if the functor
p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{C}
is a partial discrete op/bi/fibration, in the sense that for any
w:A\to B
in
\mathcal{C}
and
q\in\mathcal{Q}
such that
p(q)=A
(or
q^{\prime}
such that
p(q^{\prime})=B
), there is at most one
\alpha:q\to q^{\prime}
such that
p(\alpha)=w
.

Remark 16
.

With this way of representing finite-state automata by functors
p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{C}
,
\epsilon
-transitions may be naturally interpreted as non-identity arrows
