\alpha:q\to q^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{Q}
mapped by
p
to an identity arrow
id_{A}:A\to A
in
\mathcal{C}
.
However, it is an immediate consequence of the ULF property that an NFA in the sense of Definition
2.1
cannot contain any non-trivial fiber categories
p^{-1}(A)
(Proposition
11
).
One would therefore need a more general notion of ULF functor in order to accommodate categorical NFAs with
\epsilon
-transitions.
This seems to raise some subtle issues.
In particular, the standard treatment of
\epsilon
-removal
[
HMU07
, §2.5]
seems to indicate that one needs to design a notion between ULF functors and general Conduché fibrations.
Recall that the standard notion of Conduché fibration weakens the ULF property by only requiring uniqueness of factorizations up to zigzag in the fiber, which means here zigzag of
\epsilon
-transitions.
The right definition of categorical NFA with
\epsilon
-transitions should probably ensure more, that there is a universal (initial or terminal) solution in the space of possible factorizations.

As we have seen, our definition of NFA over a category does not require the base category
\mathcal{C}
to be free.
We now list some examples of such automata and the regular languages of arrows that they generate.

{exa}
[Product automata]
The product of two finitary ULF functors
p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{C}
and
p^{\prime}:\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is again a finitary ULF functor
p\times p^{\prime}:\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.
This enables us to construct the product of two NFAs
M
and
M^{\prime}
as an NFA
M\times M^{\prime}
whose underlying bare automaton is
p\times p^{\prime}
and whose initial and accepting states are given by the pairing of the respective initial states
(q_{0},q_{0}^{\prime})
and
(q_{f},q_{f}^{\prime})
.
Note that even if
M
and
M^{\prime}
are NFAs over free categories
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{G}}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{G}^{\prime}}
, the product automaton
M\times M^{\prime}
will not be since the product of free categories is not a free category.
Intuitively,
M\times M^{\prime}
may be interpreted as an automaton that reads pairs of arrows in parallel.
It recognizes the cartesian product
\mathcal{L}\times\mathcal{L}^{{}^{\prime}}
of the languages recognized by
M
and
M^{\prime}
.

{exa}
[Singleton automata]
For any word
w=a_{1}\dots a_{n}
of length
n
, there is a partial bideterministic
(n+1)
-state automaton
M_{w}
that recognizes the singleton language
\{\,{w}\,\}
, with initial state
0
, accepting state
n
, and transitions of the form
(i,a_{i+1},i+1)
for each
0\leq i<n
.
This construction may be generalized to define a (not necessarily deterministic or codeterministic) NFA
M_{w}
recognizing any arrow
w
in any category satisfying a certain finitariness assumption.
Indeed, for any arrow
w:A\to B
of a category
\mathcal{C}
, the
category of factorizations

\mathsf{Fact}_{w}
of
w
(also called the
interval category
of
w
in
[
LM10
]
) is defined by taking objects to be triples
(X,u,v)
of an object
X\in\mathcal{C}
and a pair of arrows
u:A\to X
,
v:X\to B
such that
w=uv
, and arrows
(X,u,v)\to(X^{\prime},u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
to be arrows
x:X\to X^{\prime}
such that
u^{\prime}=ux
and
v=xv^{\prime}
.
This category has an initial object
(id_{A},w)
and a terminal object
(w,id_{B})
, and it comes equipped with an evident forgetful functor
\mu_{w}:\mathsf{Fact}_{w}\to\mathcal{C}
returning the middle of a factorization, which is always ULF
[
Joh99
,
LM10
]
.
Hence

M_{w}=(\mathcal{C},\mathsf{Fact}_{w},\mu_{w},(id_{A},w),(w,id_{B}))

(9)

may be considered as a nondeterministic automaton recognizing the singleton language
\{\,{w}\,\}
, although it is not necessarily a finite-state automaton since the functor
\mu_{w}
need not be finitary in general.
Let us say that a category
\mathcal{C}

has finitary factorizations
if this is always the case, in other words, if the ULF functor
\mu_{w}:\mathsf{Fact}_{w}\to\mathcal{C}
is finitary for every arrow
w
of
\mathcal{C}
.
Then we see that to any arrow
w
of a category
\mathcal{C}
with finitary factorizations, (
9
) defines an NFA
M_{w}
recognizing exactly that arrow.

Free categories always have finitary factorizations, and indeed the category of factorizations of any arrow of a free category is isomorphic to a non-empty finite ordinal, as in the example above of the
(n+1)
-state automaton associated to a word of length
n
.

A category with finitely many objects has finitary factorizations just in case every arrow has finitely many factorizations
w=uv
of length 2.
4
4

4

Note that having finitary factorizations is weaker than the
Möbius
condition on a category
[
LM10
]
, which asks that the set of non-trivial decompositions
\{\,{(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})\mid n\geq 0,w=u_{1}\cdots u_{n}(\forall 1\leq i\leq n,u_{i}\neq id)}\,\}
of every arrow
w
is finite (cf.
[
LM10
, Prop. 2.6]
).
As a property of monoids, having finitary factorizations is standardly called the
finite decomposition
property, which is weaker than what Eilenberg called the
local finiteness
property
[
Eil74
, §VII.4, p.170]
corresponding to the Möbius condition.

For example, consider the one-object category
\mathcal{E}
containing a single non-identity arrow
f:*\to*
that is idempotent
ff=f
.
The NFA
M_{f}
recognizing the language
\{\,{f}\,\}\subseteq\mathcal{E}(\ast,\ast)

has three states
(id,f)
,
(f,id)
, and
(f,f)
, as depicted by the following transition diagram in which all paths of length
\geq 1
commute:

id,f
f,id
f,f
f
f
f
f
f

{exa}

[Total automata]
The identity functor
id_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
on a category is trivially ULF and finitary, since every object and arrow has a unique lifting.
Hence for any pair of objects
A
and
B
of
\mathcal{C}
, there is an NFA
M_{\mathcal{C}(A,B)}=(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C},id_{\mathcal{C}},A,B)
recognizing the total language
\mathcal{C}(A,B)
, i.e., accepting every arrow
w:A\to B
.
In the case of the one-object category
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
, this is just the familiar automaton with a single (initial and accepting) state and a transition on that state for every letter of
\Sigma
.
On the other hand, it is worth emphasizing that
M_{\mathcal{C}(A,B)}
defines an NFA in our sense even when
\mathcal{C}
has infinitely many objects.
In particular, this example shows that what is important is finiteness of the
fibers
of the functor
p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{C}
defining an NFA, and not of the total category of states
\mathcal{Q}
.

{exa}

[Asynchronous automata]
One good reason for considering NFAs whose base category
\mathcal{C}

is not free is that they provide for interesting notions of automata
when the category
\mathcal{C}
is presented by generators and relations.
Let us say that an
equation
in a graph
\mathbb{G}
is a pair
(f,g)
of paths
f,g:A\to B

with same source and target.
Every set
\mathcal{R}
of such equations
induces a congruence
\sim_{\mathcal{R}}

on the paths of
\mathbb{G}
,
defined as the smallest equivalence relation
between paths of
\mathbb{G}
with same source and target,
compatible to composition, and
such that
f\sim_{\mathcal{R}}g

for every equation
(f,g)\in\mathcal{R}
.
We say that a category
\mathcal{C}
is
presented
by a graph
\mathbb{G}

of generators and a set
\mathcal{R}
of equations
when it comes equipped with an isomorphism

c:\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{G}}/{\sim_{\,\mathcal{R}}}\to\mathcal{C}

from the category
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{G}}/{\sim_{\,\mathcal{R}}}
whose objects are nodes of
\mathbb{G}
and whose arrows are
\sim_{\,\mathcal{R}}
-equivalence classes of paths.

A remarkable property of ULF functors
p:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
into a category
\mathcal{C}

presented by a graph
\mathbb{G}
and a set
\mathcal{R}
of equations,
with isomorphism
c:\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{G}}/{\sim_{\,\mathcal{R}}}\to\mathcal{C}
,
is that the category
\mathcal{D}
inherits a presentation by a graph
\mathop{p^{*}}\mathbb{G}

and a set
\mathop{p^{*}}\mathcal{R}
of equations derived from
\mathbb{G}
and
\mathcal{R}
.
The graph
\mathop{p^{*}}\mathbb{G}
has nodes the pairs
(x,A)

of a node
x
in
\mathbb{G}
and an object
A
of
\mathcal{D}

such that
c(x)=p(A)
, and edges the pairs
(e,u)
of an edge
e:x\to y
in
\mathbb{G}

and an arrow
u:A\to B
in
\mathcal{D}
such that
c(e)=p(u)
.
The set
\mathop{p^{*}}\mathcal{R}
contains all equations
(f,g)

between paths of
\mathop{p^{*}}\mathbb{G}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f=(e_{1},u_{1})\cdots(e_{m},u_{m})\,,\,g=(e^{\prime}_{1},v_{1})\cdots(e^{\prime}_{n},v_{n})\quad:\quad(x,A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(y,B)}

such that
(e_{1}\cdots e_{m},e^{\prime}_{1}\cdots e^{\prime}_{n})\in\mathcal{R}

and the composite arrows

u_{1}\cdots u_{m}=v_{1}\cdots v_{n}

are equal in the category
\mathcal{D}
.
The isomorphism
d:\mathop{p^{*}}\mathbb{G}/{\sim_{\,\mathop{p^{*}}\mathcal{R}}}\to\mathcal{D}
is given by the second projection
(x,A)\mapsto A
on objects, and by the map
[e_{1}\cdots e_{m},u_{1}\cdots u_{m}]\mapsto u_{1}\cdots u_{m}
sending an equivalence class of lists of edge/arrow pairs to the composition of the arrows in
\mathcal{D}
.

We illustrate the property by giving a direct description of NFAs
on a category
\mathcal{C}
defined by a Mazurkiewicz alphabet.
A concurrent alphabet
(\Sigma,\mathit{D})

as defined by Mazurkiewicz
[
Maz89
]

is a set
\Sigma
of letters, equipped with a reflexive
and symmetric binary relation
\mathit{D}\subseteq\Sigma\times\Sigma
.
Two letters
a,b\in\Sigma
are called
dependent
when
(a,b)\in\mathit{D}

and
independent
otherwise.
The
permutation equivalence
on finite words is defined as the smallest
equivalence relation
{\sim}

such that
w_{1}\,a\,b\,w_{2}\sim w_{1}\,b\,a\,w_{2}
for every pair of words

w_{1},w_{2}\in\Sigma^{\ast}
and independent letters
a,b\in\Sigma
.
A
trace
is an equivalence class of the permutation equivalence.
Traces
w\in\Sigma^{\ast}\!/\!\sim
may be regarded
as the arrows
w:\ast\to\ast
of a one-object category
\mathcal{F}\,{{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}}/{\sim_{\,\mathit{D}}}

obtained by quotienting the category
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}

with an equation between arrows
a\,b=b\,a:\ast\to\ast

for every pair of independent letters
a,b\in\Sigma
.

Unpacking the definitions, a NFA over
\mathcal{F}\,{{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}}/{\sim_{\,\mathit{D}}}

may be equivalently described as follows, starting from the data of
a finite set
Q
of states, a finite set
\mathit{Tran}
of transitions
together with a labelling function
\delta:\mathit{Tran}\to Q\times\Sigma\times Q
,
and a choice of initial and accepting states.
The transition graph is moreover equipped with a set
\diamond
of permutation tiles
defined as pairs
(\alpha,\beta)
of paths
\alpha=st
and
\beta=t^{\prime}s^{\prime}

of length
2
of the form

\delta(s)=(q,a,q^{\prime})
,
\delta(t)=(q^{\prime},b,q^{\prime\prime})
,

\delta(t^{\prime})=(q,b,q^{\prime\prime\prime})
and
\delta(s^{\prime})=(q^{\prime\prime\prime},a,q^{\prime\prime})

where
q,q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime\prime\prime}\in Q
are states and the two letters
a,b\in\Sigma
are independent.
We write
\alpha\diamond\beta
when
(\alpha,\beta)\in\diamond
.
The set of permutation tiles is required to satisfy the following properties:

(1)

permutation tiles are deterministic:

\alpha\diamond\beta
and
\alpha\diamond\beta^{\prime}
implies
\beta=\beta^{\prime}
,

(2)

permutation tiles are symmetric:

\alpha\diamond\beta
implies
\beta\diamond\alpha
,

(3)

successive transitions with independent letters can permute:
for every transition path
\alpha=s\,t
of length
2

with
\delta(s)=(q,a,q^{\prime})
and
\delta(t)=(q^{\prime},b,q^{\prime\prime})

where the two letters
a,b\in\Sigma
are independent,
there exists a permutation tile
\alpha\diamond\beta
.

The correspondence works as follows.
Given a finite transition system
(Q,\mathit{Tran},\delta)

equipped with a set
\diamond
of permutation tiles,
one defines the category
\mathcal{C}
presented
by the corresponding graph
\mathbb{G}

with an equation
(\alpha,\beta)
for every permutation tile
\alpha\diamond\beta
;
the three properties (1), (2), and (3) ensure then that
the labelling functor
q:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{F}\,{{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}}/{\sim_{\,\mathit{D}}}

is finitary ULF.
Conversely, every finitary ULF functor

p:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{F}\,{{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}}/{\sim_{\,\mathit{D}}}

induces a finite graph
\mathbb{G}=\mathop{p^{*}}{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}

and a set of equations
\diamond=\mathop{p^{*}}\mathcal{R}_{\mathit{D}}

where
\mathcal{R}_{\mathit{D}}
denotes the set of equations

(ab,ba)
for
a
and
b
two independent letters ;
one checks that the nodes and edges of
\mathbb{G}

define a transition system
(Q,\mathit{Tran},\delta)

which satisfies properties (1), (2), and (3).
Note that property (3) reflects the ULF property
for the factorisation
of the arrow
ab
in
\mathcal{F}\,{{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}}/{\sim_{\,\mathit{D}}}
as
b
followed by
a
,
for any pair of independent letters
a
and
b
in
\Sigma
.

This establishes that the notion of NFA over
the category
\mathcal{F}\,{{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}}/{\sim_{\,\mathit{D}}}

of Mazurkiewicz traces captures a natural notion of asynchronous automaton,
corresponding to a nondeterministic version of Bednarczyk’s
asynchronous systems
[
Bed88
,
HM00
,
Mor05
,
BM06
]

where property (3) is called the Independent Diamond (ID) property.
A detailed comparison of this class of asynchronous automaton
with the more liberal notion of automaton with concurrency relations
[
DS02
,
GM10
]
appears in
[
Mor05
]
.
The ID property implies that
every NFA over
\mathcal{F}\,{{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}}/\mathit{D}

satisfies the cube property (CP) discussed in
[
HM00
]

and thus defines what is called an asynchronous graph in
[
MM07
,
Mel21
]
.
We leave for future work the connection with Zielonka’s theorem
which is based on a more concrete and process-based notion
of concurrent automaton
[
Zie87
,
BM06
]
.

2.2.
From word automata to tree automata and beyond

Reassuringly, the foregoing fibrational analysis of nondeterministic finite-state automata based on finitary ULF functors adapts smoothly when one shifts from word automata to tree automata
[
CDG+08
]
.
As a first step in that direction, we describe how the ULF and finite fiber properties may be extended to functors of operads.

{defi}

A functor of operads
p:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{O}
has the
unique lifting of factorizations property
(or
is ULF
) if any of the following equivalent conditions hold:

(1)

for any operation
\alpha
of
\mathcal{D}
, if
p(\alpha)=g\circ(h_{1},\dots,h_{n})
for some operation
g
and list of operations
h_{1},\dots,h_{n}
of
\mathcal{O}
, there exists a unique operation
\beta
and list of operations
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
of
\mathcal{D}
such that
\alpha=\beta\circ(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})
and
p(\beta)=g
,
p(\gamma_{1})=h_{1},\dots,p(\gamma_{n})=h_{n}
;

(2)

for any operation
\alpha
of
\mathcal{D}
, if
p(\alpha)=g\circ_{i}h
for some operations
g
and
h
of
\mathcal{O}
and index
i
, there exists a unique pair of operations
\beta
and
\gamma
of
\mathcal{D}
such that
\alpha=\beta\circ_{i}\gamma
and
p(\beta)=g
and
p(\gamma)=h
;

(3)

the structure maps of the associated lax functor of operads
F:\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
discussed in §
1.5
are invertible.

{defi}

We say that a functor of operads
p:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{O}
is
finitary
if either of the following equivalent conditions hold:

•

the fiber
p^{-1}(A)
as well as the fiber
p^{-1}(f)
is finite for every color
A
and operation
f
of the operad
\mathcal{O}
;

•

the associated lax functor of operads
F:\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
factors via
\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{FinSet})
.

Just as we saw in the previous section, one can easily check that the underlying bare automaton
M=(\Sigma,Q,\delta)
of a (bottom-up or top-down) nondeterministic finite-state tree automaton (cf. §1.1 and §1.6 of
[
CDG+08
]
) gives rise to a finitary ULF functor of operads
p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{F}\,\Sigma
,
where

•

\mathcal{F}\,\Sigma
is the free operad generated by the ranked alphabet
\Sigma
(seen as an uncolored species);

•

the operad
\mathcal{Q}
has states of the automaton as colors and operations freely generated by
n
-ary nodes of the form
t:q_{1},\dots,q_{n}\to q
for every transition
t\in\mathit{Tran}
of the form
\delta(t)=(q_{1},\dots,q_{n},a,q)
, with
a\in\Sigma_{n}
;

•

the functor
p
transports every such
n
-ary transition
t:q_{1},\dots,q_{n}\to q
to the underlying
n
-ary letter
a:\ast,\dots,\ast\to\ast
.

Moreover, this correspondence is one-to-one.

Proposition 17
.

Let
p:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{O}
be a functor into an operad
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{F}\,\Sigma
freely generated by some species
\Sigma
.
Then
p
is ULF iff
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
is free over some species
\mathbb{S}
and
p=\mathcal{F}\,\phi
is generated by a map of species
\phi:\mathbb{S}\to\Sigma
.

Proposition 18
.

A functor of operads
p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{F}\,\Sigma
corresponds to a bare nondeterministic finite-state tree automaton
iff
p
is ULF and finitary.

This motivates us to proceed as for word automata and propose a more general notion
of finite-state automaton over an arbitrary operad.

{defi}

A
nondeterministic finite-state automaton over an operad
is given by a tuple

M=(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{Q},p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{O},q_{r})

consisting of two operads
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{Q}
, a finitary ULF functor of operads
p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{O}
,
and a color
q_{r}
of
\mathcal{Q}
.
A color of
\mathcal{Q}
is called a
state
, and an operation of
\mathcal{Q}
is called a
run tree
of the automaton
p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{O}
.
The
regular language of constants

\mathcal{L}_{M}
recognized by the automaton
is the set of constants
c
in
\mathcal{O}
that can be lifted along
p
to a constant
\alpha:q_{r}
in
\mathcal{Q}
, that is
\mathcal{L}_{M}=\{\,{p(\alpha)\mid\alpha:q_{r}}\,\}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(A)
, where
p(q_{r})=A
.

Let us refer to NFAs over categories in the sense of Definition
2.1
as
categorical automata
for short, and NFAs over operads in the foregoing sense as
operadic automata
.

We now state a simple property of ULF and finitary functors establishing a useful connection
between categorical automata and operadic automata.

Proposition 19
.

Suppose that
p:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{C}
is a functor of categories.
If
p
is ULF (respectively, finitary) then so is the functor of operads
\mathcal{W}\,{p}:\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{Q}}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proof 2.6
.

Follows immediately from the fact that a lifting of an operation
f_{0}{-}\dots{-}f_{n}
in
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
along
\mathcal{W}\,{p}
is a sequence of liftings of the arrows
f_{0},\dots,f_{n}
in
\mathcal{C}
along
p
.

Corollary 20
.

For any categorical automaton
M=(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Q},p,q_{0},q_{f})
there is an associated operadic automaton
\mathcal{W}\,{M}=(\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}},\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{Q}},\mathcal{W}\,{p},(q_{0},q_{f}))
generating the same language
\mathcal{L}_{M}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{W}\,{M}}
.

Proof 2.7
.

By Proposition
19
, and because constants of
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
are exactly arrows of
\mathcal{C}
.

We will see that the
\mathcal{W}\,{M}
construction transforming an automaton over a category into an automaton over its operad of spliced arrows is fundamental for computing the intersection of a context-free language with a regular language of arrows.
Moreover, we highlight that since operads of spliced arrows are not freely generated (Remark
1
), the
\mathcal{W}\,{M}
construction provides an important example of how operadic automata truly generalize classical tree automata.

Remark 21
.

As an aside, we mention that it is also interesting to consider
deterministic
finite-state automata over spliced arrow operads.
To see why, let us first observe that any discrete opfibration over
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
, which may be viewed equivalently as a functor of operads
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathrm{Set}
, induces a category
\mathcal{D}
equipped with an identity-on-objects functor of categories
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
, and conversely.
Indeed, given a functor
F:\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathrm{Set}
one can construct a new category
\mathcal{D}_{F}
with same objects as
\mathcal{C}
and whose arrows
\alpha:A\to B
are given by the elements of
F(A,B)
, with composition and identities defined by the interpretations

\displaystyle F(id_{A}{-}id_{B}{-}id_{C})

\displaystyle:F(A,B)\times F(B,C)\longrightarrow F(A,C)

\displaystyle F(id_{A})

\displaystyle:F(A,A)

of the corresponding spliced arrow operations.
Moreover, there is an identity-on-objects functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}_{F}
given by sending every arrow
w:A\to B
of
\mathcal{C}
, seen as a constant
w:(A,B)
of
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
, to its interpretation
F(w):F(A,B)
.
Conversely, given a category
\mathcal{D}
and an identity-on-objects functor
h:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
, one can define
F_{h}:\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathrm{Set}
by taking
F_{h}(A,B)=\mathcal{D}(A,B)
and defining the interpretation of the spliced arrow operations by mapping the arrows of
\mathcal{C}
into
\mathcal{D}
along
h
and then composing:

F_{h}(w_{0}{-}w_{1}\dots{-}w_{n})=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})\mapsto h(w_{0})u_{1}h(w_{1})\dots u_{n}h(w_{n})

From the description of this correspondence, it is clear it restricts to one between
finitary
discrete opfibrations over
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
(equivalent to functors
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathrm{FinSet}
) and
locally finite
categories
\mathcal{D}
equipped with an identity-on-objects functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
.
Thus what is a DFA over
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
?
Nothing more than a locally finite category
\mathcal{D}
, an identity-on-objects functor
h:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
, and an arrow
q_{r}:A\to B
of
\mathcal{D}
, with the DFA accepting an arrow
w:A\to B
of
\mathcal{C}
just in case
h(w)=q_{r}
.
In the case that
\mathcal{C}
is a one-object category, this is essentially the notion of
recognition by a finite monoid,
which is one of the classical equivalent characterizations of regular languages
[
Eil74
, §III.10, p.62]
.
5
5

5

A language
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
is said to be recognizable by a finite monoid
X
if there exists a subset
S\subseteq X
and a homomorphism
h:\Sigma^{*}\to X
such that
\mathcal{L}=h^{-1}(S)
. Our formulation requires
S
to be a singleton, cf. Proposition
15
above and the preceding discussion.

2.3.
Additional properties of ULF and finitary functors

We state here a few more basic properties of ULF and finitary functors, in both categorical and operadic versions, that will be important in the rest of the paper.

Proposition 22
.

If
p:Y\to X
is a ULF (resp. finitary) functor of categories or operads, and
G:Z\to X
is an arbitrary functor, then the functor
\mathop{G^{*}}p:Z\times_{X}Y\to Z
defined by pulling back
p
along
G
is ULF (resp. finitary).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z\times_{X}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{G^{*}}p\ \text{ULF/finitary}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\ \text{ULF/finitary}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

Closure of both classes of functors under pullback along arbitrary functors may be verified directly, or may be seen as an immediate consequence of the fact that both classes of functors
p:Y\to X
admit “Grothendieck-type” correspondences with pseudo or lax functors
F:X\to\mathcal{U}
into a certain universe (namely, ULF functors as pseudo functors into
\mathcal{U}=\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
, and finitary functors as lax functors into
\mathcal{U}=\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{FinSet})
), so that the pullback along an ordinary functor
G:Z\to X
is simply the composite
F\circ G:Z\to\mathcal{U}
.

As we already saw in Example
2.1
, the identity functor
id_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
on any category is trivially finitary and ULF, as is the identity functor
id_{\mathcal{O}}:\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{O}
on any operad.
It is also easy to check that both classes of functors are closed under composition.
Moreover, ULF functors satisfy a property analogous to the pasting law for pullbacks in a category, while finitary functors behave similarly to monomorphisms.

Proposition 23
.

Consider a commutative triangle of functors of categories or operads:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

(1)

if
F
and
G
are ULF, then so is
H

(2)

if
F
and
G
are finitary, then so is
H

(3)

if
G
and
H
are ULF then so is
F

(4)

if
H
is finitary then so is
F

Proof 2.8
.

For concreteness, we take
X
,
Y
, and
Z
to be categories. (The case where they are operads is completely analogous.)

(1)

Let
\alpha
be an arrow in
X
. Any factorization
H(\alpha)=uv
in
Z
lifts uniquely to a factorization of
\alpha=\beta\gamma
with
H(\beta)=u
,
H(\gamma)=v
, by first lifting along
G
to obtain a unique factorization of
F(\alpha)
, and then lifting along
F
.

(2)

All of the fibers of
H
are finite (for both the objects and arrows of
Z
) since they may be decomposed as a finite union of finite fibers

H^{-1}(z)=\bigcup_{y\in G^{-1}(z)}F^{-1}(y)
.

(3)

Let
\alpha
be an arrow in
X
and
F(\alpha)=st
a factorization in
Y
.
By projecting down along
G
and then lifting the resulting factorization
H(\alpha)=G(s)G(t)
along
H
, we obtain unique
\beta
and
\gamma
such that
\alpha=\beta\gamma
,
H(\beta)=G(s)
,
H(\gamma)=G(t)
.
But since
F(\alpha)=F(\beta)F(\gamma)
is a lifting of the factorization
G(st)=G(s)G(t)
along
G
, this implies that
F(\beta)=s
and
F(\gamma)=t
by the ULF property of
G
.

(4)

All of the fibers
F^{-1}(y)
are finite, since they are contained in the fibers
H^{-1}(G(y))
.

We remark in passing that finitary ULF functors may be arranged into various categories and bicategories of interest, which we describe now for the case of categorical automata (the operadic case again being completely analogous), although we will not make explicit use of these (bi)categories in the paper.
For any category
\mathcal{C}
, finitary ULF functors over
\mathcal{C}
and commutative triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
finULF
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
finULF

define a category of bare NFAs over
\mathcal{C}
and morphisms between them, where the functor
F:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
may be seen as a
functional simulation
since it sends any run
\alpha:q_{0}\to q_{f}
in
\mathcal{Q}
over an arrow
w:A\to B
of
\mathcal{C}
to a run
F(\alpha):F(q_{0})\to F(q_{f})
in
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
over the same arrow.
By Proposition
23
,
F
is itself finitary ULF, and this category is just the slice
\mathrm{finULF}/\mathcal{C}
of the category
\mathrm{finULF}
of small categories and finitary ULF functors between them.
It is possible to formulate an analogue of Proposition
6
: if
M
and
M^{\prime}
are two NFAs over the same category, and
F:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is a functional simulation between their underlying bare automata that preserves initial and accepting states, then
\mathcal{L}_{M}\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{M^{\prime}}
, where equality holds
\mathcal{L}_{M}=\mathcal{L}_{M^{\prime}}
when
F
is full, extending to an isomorphism of runs when
F
is fully faithful.

In a different direction of abstraction, a span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
finULF
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O}

of a finitary ULF functor
\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{C}
and an arbitrary functor
O:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{D}
may be considered as a
nondeterministic finite-state transducer

\mathcal{C}{\,\longrightarrow\hskip-11.49995pt|\hskip 9.49997pt}\mathcal{D}
, since it associates to every run
\alpha:q_{0}\to q_{f}
in
\mathcal{Q}
over an arrow
w:A\to B
of
\mathcal{C}
a corresponding output arrow
O(\alpha):O(q_{0})\to O(q_{f})
in
\mathcal{D}
.
By Propositions
22
and
23
, ordinary composition of spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}\times_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
finULF

\lrcorner

finULF
finULF

combines a transducer
\mathcal{C}{\,\longrightarrow\hskip-11.49995pt|\hskip 9.49997pt}\mathcal{D}
with a transducer
\mathcal{D}{\,\longrightarrow\hskip-11.49995pt|\hskip 9.49997pt}\mathcal{E}
to produce a transducer
\mathcal{C}{\,\longrightarrow\hskip-11.49995pt|\hskip 9.49997pt}\mathcal{E}
, and one obtains a bicategory by defining a 2-cell between transducers of the same type as a functional simulation between the underlying bare automata that commutes with the output functors.

2.4.
Closure properties of regular languages

Closure of finitary ULF functors under pullback along arbitrary functors (Proposition
22
) implies that regular languages of arrows and constants are closed under inverse functorial image, restricted to a suitable choice of objects in the source category or operad.

Proposition 24
.

Let
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(A,B)
be a regular language of arrows in
\mathcal{C}
, let
F:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
be a functor of categories, and let
R,S
be objects of
\mathcal{D}
such that
F(R)=A,F(S)=B
.
Then the homset-restricted inverse image
F^{-1}(\mathcal{L})\cap\mathcal{D}(R,S)
is regular.

Proposition 25
.

Let
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(A)
be a regular language of constants in
\mathcal{O}
, let
F:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{O}
be a functor of operads, and let
R
be a color of
\mathcal{P}
such that
F(R)=A
.
Then the conset-restricted inverse image
F^{-1}(\mathcal{L})\cap\mathcal{P}(R)
is regular.

Note that restricting the inverse image to a homset/conset is necessary due to our conventions for the initial and accepting states of categorical and operadic automata (although variations of those conventions could certainly be envisaged, see discussion above after Def.
2.1
).
Indeed, given a categorical automaton
M=(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Q},p,q_{0},q_{f})
, a functor
F:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
, and a pair of objects
R,S
of
\mathcal{D}
such that
F(R)=p(q_{0}),F(S)=p(q_{f})
, the restricted pullback automaton is given by
\mathop{F^{*}}M|_{R,S}=(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}\times_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{Q},\mathop{F^{*}}p,(R,q_{0}),(S,q_{f}))
.
The restricted pullback of an operadic automaton is constructed similarly.

Conversely, since finitary ULF functors are closed under composition (Proposition
23
), we immediately obtain closure of regular languages under functorial image along a finitary ULF functor.

Proposition 26
.

If
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(A,B)
is a regular language of arrows in
\mathcal{C}
and
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a finitary ULF functor of categories, then the image
F(\mathcal{L})\subseteq\mathcal{D}(F(A),F(B))
is also regular.

Proposition 27
.

If
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(A)
is a regular language of constants in
\mathcal{O}
and
F:\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{P}
is a finitary ULF functor of operads, then the image
F(\mathcal{L})\subseteq\mathcal{P}(F(A))
is also regular.

For example, the pushforward of a categorical automaton
M=(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Q},p,q_{0},q_{f})
along a finitary ULF functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is the automaton
\mathop{F_{*}}M=(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{Q},F\circ p,q_{0},q_{f})
.

On the other hand, regular languages are
not
closed under image along an arbitrary functor, as the following counterexamples demonstrate.

Counterexample 28
.

Let
\mathcal{W}
be the
category of Dyck walks
freely generated by the graph whose nodes are natural numbers and with a pair of edges

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{n}}

for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Let
\Sigma
be the alphabet containing a pair of brackets ‘
[
’ and ‘
]
’ and consider the functor
F:\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
defined by

n\mapsto*\qquad u_{n}\mapsto\mathop{[}\qquad d_{n}\mapsto\mathop{]}

for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
The total language
\mathcal{W}(0,0)
is regular (Example
2.1
), but its image
F(\mathcal{W}(0,0))\subseteq\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}(*,*)
is the Dyck language of well-bracketed words, which is not a regular language in the classical sense
[
HMU07
, p. 195]
and hence cannot be a regular language of arrows in our sense by Prop.
14
.
Observe in this case that the functor
F
is ULF since it is generated from a graph homomorphism, but it is manifestly not finitary.

.

be the walking arrow category, and consider any functor

for some unique arrows
\beta_{n}
and
\gamma_{n}
lying over
\frac{1}{n}
and
\frac{n-1}{n}
, by assumption that
p
is ULF. Now there are two possibilities:

(1)

The objects
q_{n}
are distinct for all
n\geq 1
. In this case the fiber
p^{-1}(*)
is infinite, contradicting the assumption that
p
is finitary.

(2)

There exist
i<j
such that
q_{i}=q_{j}
. In this case

is another accepting run of the automaton over the element
p(\alpha^{\prime})=\frac{1}{i}+\frac{j-1}{j}>1
, contradicting the assumption that
M
recognizes just the singleton language
\{\,{1}\,\}
.

Finally, we can easily derive that regular languages are closed under intersection.

Proposition 30
.

If
\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(A,B)
(resp.
\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(A)
) are regular languages of arrows (resp. constants), then so is their intersection
\mathcal{L}_{1}\cap\mathcal{L}_{2}
.

Proof 2.9
.

Given two automata
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
with underlying bare automata
p_{1}:\mathcal{Q}_{1}\to\mathcal{C}
and
p_{2}:\mathcal{Q}_{2}\to\mathcal{C}
over the same category
\mathcal{C}
, and with initial and accepting states mapped to the same pair of objects
A
and
B
, the intersection automaton
M_{1}\cap M_{2}
can be constructed in either of two equivalent ways:

(1)

By pulling back
p_{1}
along
p_{2}
, and then taking the image along
p_{2}
.

(2)

By forming the product automaton
M_{1}\times M_{2}
(Example
2.1
) over
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}
and then pulling back along the diagonal functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}
.

The construction of the intersection of operadic automata is completely analogous.

At this point, we should mention that we expect regular languages of arrows and constants to satisfy some additional closure properties just as in the classical case, including suitably formulated versions of closure under union, concatenation, and Kleene star.
However, the usual proof of these facts is greatly facilitated by the use of
\epsilon
-transitions, which are ruled out by our working definition of categorical and operadic NFAs based on ULF functors (see Remark
16
).
We therefore defer consideration of these additional closure properties to the future introduction of an appropriate notion of categorical/operadic automaton with
\epsilon
-transitions.
In any case, we will not need them to prove the representation theorem.

2.5.
Related work

It is standard to represent finite-state automata by their transition graphs, and natural to consider such transition graphs as ordinary (finite directed) graphs equipped with a homomorphism into the bouquet graph on the underlying alphabet.
Such a perspective on nondeterministic finite-state automata without
\epsilon
-transitions was developed by Steinberg
[
Ste01
]
, motivated by monoid and semigroup theory, and with special prominence given to graph homomorphisms that are
immersions,
which correspond to partial bideterministic automata in the sense of Definition
2.1
. Walters’ short note on context-free languages
[
Wal89
]
that we mentioned in §
1.9
also opens with a treatment of automata as graph homomorphisms, but taking morphisms of
reflexive graphs
in order to allow
\epsilon
-transitions.
As discussed in Remark
16
, an important question is how to adapt our approach based on finitary ULF functors to allow for
\epsilon
-transitions, although we are confident that this can be done by appropriately generalizing the notion of ULF.
Walters’ approach to automata and context-free grammars was further developed by Rosenthal
[
Ros95
]
, considering also tree automata, and more recently by Earnshaw and Sobociński
[
ES22
]
to define regular languages in free monoidal categories.

The fibrational perspective on nondeterministic finite-state automata over categories and operads that we introduced here is closely related to the “functorial approach” to automata theory developed by Colcombet and Petrişan
[
CP20
]
.
Roughly speaking, the approaches are dual and related by Grothendieck-type correspondences: whereas we consider automata as functors into a category (or operad) with certain fibrational properties, Colcombet and Petrişan consider them as functors
out of
a category into different large categories of interest, such as
\mathrm{Set}
for deterministic automata,
\mathrm{Rel}
for nondeterministic automata, or
\mathrm{Vec}
for weighted automata.
Given that in many cases such Grothendieck-type correspondences rise to the level of equivalences, generally the approaches provide two complementary perspectives on the same objects.
Still, it is worth drawing attention to a few small but important differences between our approach and the approach described in
[
CP20
]
.
First, although many of the definitions and constructions given by Colcombet and Petrişan are stated for an arbitrary “input category”
\mathcal{I}
, in practice they consider automata over a fixed category
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{word}}
, isomorphic to the category we call
\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}^{\textrm{\^{}}\$}}
, and they do not impose any finiteness condition on automata.
Second, rather than having a single initial and accepting state, Colcombet and Petrişan take a more abstract definition of the “behavior” of an automaton given by precomposing with a full and faithful functor
\iota:\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{I}
from some “output category”
\mathcal{O}
, typically the full subcategory of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{word}}\cong\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}^{\textrm{\^{}}\$}}
spanned by the arrows
\bot\to\top
.
The idea is that precomposing with
\iota
abstracts away the internal states of the automaton, remembering just the language that it recognizes.
Colcombet and Petrişan use this to give an elegant analysis of automata minimization based on factorization systems.

q_{0}
q_{1}
q_{2}
q_{f}
a
a
b
b

Finally, and although it is by no means forced by the functorial approach, let us observe that modelling nondeterministic automata as functors into
\mathrm{Rel}
(rather than as pseudofunctors into
\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Set})
or
\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{FinSet})
) means equating all of the runs over a given word between a given pair of states.
For instance, the NFA shown on the left has two distinct runs
q_{0}\to q_{f}
over the word
ab
, but these are identified when considering it as a
\mathrm{Rel}
-automaton.

The idea of generalizing regular languages from recognizable subsets of free monoids
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
to recognizable subsets of arbitrary monoids
\mathcal{L}\subseteq X
is classic.
For instance, it is discussed in Eilenberg’s book (see
[
Eil74
, §III.12, p.68]
) using several of the equivalent characterizations of regular languages, including recognition by a finite monoid. He mentions that the definition of automaton could also be suitably generalized, but does not do so explicitly “since all the known properties of recognizable sets already follow from the definition adopted above”.
There is also precedent for the idea of generalizing from languages of words in free monoids to languages of arrows in free categories, having been proposed at least as early as the mid-1980s in the work of Thérien and collaborators
[
TSG88
,
WT86
]
, who argued that this was well-motivated by automata-theoretic considerations:

For example, we are often interested in decompositions of automata.
In such situations a component may receive its input from the output of some other component.
This “preprocessing” imposes restrictions on the possible input sequences that need to be considered.
A simple way to take into account these restrictions is to view a machine as processing input sequences that are paths in a finite directed multigraph.
[
TSG88
, p.395]

Again, though, rather than taking NFAs or DFAs as a starting point, these authors started from the alternative characterization of regular word languages as unions of equivalence classes of a finite index congruence on a free monoid, and explained how to generalize it to free categories in a way that allowed proving Kleene’s theorem among other results.
We take one more opportunity to emphasize that our definition of regular language is not restricted to free categories and operads, and that non-free categories and operads are required in some key examples.
(The work by Thérien et al. was, incidentally, revisited by Jones who took a similar approach to define regular languages in a certain class of
profinite categories

[
Jon96
]
.)
We do
not
expect Kleene’s theorem on the equivalence between regular and rational languages to hold for regular languages of arrows in arbitrary categories – indeed classically it is a theorem about recognizable subsets of free monoids with counterexamples in the non-free case
[
Eil74
, §IV.5, p.175–178]
, and Counterexample
29
demonstrates that some singleton languages of arrows cannot be recognized by any NFA.
On the other hand, we mention that Fahrenberg et al. have recently proved a Kleene theorem for higher-dimensional automata (without
\epsilon
-transitions) recognizing languages of interval pomsets
[
FJSZ22
]
, which it would be interesting to analyze within our framework.

Although it is beyond the scope of this paper, we expect that regular languages of arrows or constants in the sense of Definitions
2.1
and
2.2
should also have other equivalent characterizations related to the aforementioned classical ones – in particular there seem to be natural notions of
recognition by a locally finite category
and
recognition by a locally finite operad
(cf. Remark
21
).
It may be fruitful to connect this with Salvati’s notion of regular language of
\lambda
-terms
[
Sal09
]
(in part inspired by
[
dG01
]
), which may be formulated as recognition by a locally finite and well-pointed cartesian closed category (cf.
[
MN24
]
).

Finally, we only briefly touched upon the subject of constructing categories of automata and transducers in §
2.3
, but this also has precedent in the literature.
Our definition of a nondeterministic finite-state transducer as a span
\mathcal{C}\twoheadleftarrow\mathcal{Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
where the left leg satisfies a fibrational condition (namely, is finitary and ULF) is similar to the notion of “Mealy morphism” defined by Paré using slightly different conditions
[
Par12
]
. Another line of work by Katis, Sabadini, and Walters
[
KSW97
]
models automata as spans of graphs, with sequential composition given by composition of spans.
This graph-theoretic approach is certainly related to ours, since spans of graphs are in one-to-one correspondence with spans of ULF functors between the associated free categories (Prop.
12
), and moreover the two notions of composition agree.
Hyland defined a
traced monoidal category

\mathrm{Aut}
whose objects are natural numbers and whose morphisms
m\to n
are finite-state automata (over a fixed alphabet
\Sigma
) equipped with
m
distinct initial states and
n
distinct accepting states
[
Hyl08
]
.
He then exhibited a traced monoidal functor
\mathrm{Aut}\to\mathrm{Rat}
to a category whose arrows are matrices of rational languages, as one direction of Kleene’s theorem.
It could be interesting to adapt Hyland’s analysis to our setting.

3.
The Chomsky-Schützenberger Representation Theorem

In this section, we give a relatively simple and conceptual proof of the representation theorem, generalized to context-free languages of arrows in an arbitrary category.
We begin in §
3.1
by explaining how to represent the intersection of a context-free language with a regular language by first taking the
pullback
of a CFG along an NFA.
Next, in §
3.2
, we exhibit a left adjoint to the spliced arrow operad construction, which we call the
contour category
construction.
As a direct consequence of this adjunction, we show in §
3.3
that every pointed finite species induces a CFG that is universal in a precise sense and that generates a language of
tree contour words,
which are closely related to Dyck words.
Finally, in §
3.4
we state and prove an appropriate generalization of the representation theorem, which also relies in a crucial way on the strong translation principle.

3.1.
Pulling back context-free grammars along finite-state automata

To compute the pullback, we apply two lemmas.

Lemma 31
.

Suppose given a species
\mathbb{S}
, a functor of operads
{p:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{O}}

and a ULF functor of operads
p_{\mathcal{Q}}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{O}
.
In that case, the pullback of
p
along
p_{\mathcal{Q}}
in the category of operads is obtained from
a corresponding pullback of
\phi:\mathbb{S}\to{\mathcal{O}}
along
{p_{\mathcal{Q}}}:{\mathcal{Q}}\to{\mathcal{O}}
in the category of species:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ \ \in\ \ \mathrm{Oper}

Proof 3.1
.

By Proposition
22
, the pullback of the ULF functor
p_{\mathcal{Q}}
along the functor of operads
p
is a ULF functor
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
, which must be of the form
\mathcal{F}\,\psi^{\prime}:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
for some map of species
\psi^{\prime}:\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{S}
by Proposition
17
.
We therefore have a pullback in
\mathrm{Oper}
as on the right above, which is sent to a pullback in
\mathrm{Spec}
by the right adjoint forgetful functor.
The unit of the adjunction induces another pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathbb{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathbb{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\,\psi^{\prime}}

and by composing these

\hbox to103.42pt{\vbox to87.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 51.71103pt\lower-42.40656pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-51.71103pt}{-35.21494pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -71.55 -48.73)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

we obtain the desired pullback in the category of species.

Lemma 32
.

Consider a pullback in the category of species:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

If
\mathbb{S}
is finite and
\psi
is finitary (i.e., has finite fibers) then
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
is finite.

Proof 3.2
.

The pullback species
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}=\mathbb{S}\times_{\mathbb{T}}\mathbb{T}^{\prime}
admits a simple description: its colors (resp. nodes) are pairs of colors (resp. nodes) from
\mathbb{S}
and
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}
lying over the same color (resp. node) in
\mathbb{T}
.
Hence finiteness of
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
follows immediately from the assumption that
\mathbb{S}
is finite and that
\psi
has finite fibers.

Theorem 33
.

For any CFG
G=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S},S,p_{G})
and NFA
M=(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Q},p_{M},q_{0},q_{f})
over the same category, with
p_{G}(S)=(p_{M}(q_{0}),p_{M}(q_{f}))
, there is a CFG
G^{\prime}=\mathop{M^{*}}G
generating the language
\mathcal{L}_{G^{\prime}}=p_{M}^{-1}(\mathcal{L}_{G})\cap\mathcal{Q}(q_{0},q_{f})
.

Proof 3.3
.

By Prop.
19
, the finitary ULF functor of categories
p_{M}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{C}
induces a finitary ULF functor of operads
{\mathcal{W}\,{p_{M}}}:\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{Q}}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
.
By Lemma
31
, the pullback of
p_{G}
along
p_{M}
may therefore be computed as a pullback of the underlying maps of species:

{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-10.43968pt}{-23.29913pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -14.45 -32.24)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ \ \in\ \ \mathrm{Oper}

Moreover, by Lemma
32
, the pullback species
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}=\mathbb{S}\times_{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}}\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{Q}}
is finite,
so that
\mathop{M^{*}}G=(\mathcal{Q},\mathbb{S}^{\prime},(S,(q_{0},q_{f})),p^{\prime}_{G})
defines a CFG.
Finally, we have
\mathcal{L}_{G^{\prime}}=p_{M}^{-1}(\mathcal{L}_{G})\cap\mathcal{Q}(q_{0},q_{f})
because by the universal property of the pullback in
\mathrm{Oper}
,
G^{\prime}
derives a run
\alpha:q_{0}\to q_{f}
of
M
just in case there exists a constant
\beta:S
in
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
such that
p_{G}(\beta)=p_{M}(\alpha)
.

We emphasize that the pullback grammar
\mathop{M^{*}}G
generates a language of arrows in
\mathcal{Q}
, corresponding to the runs of the automaton
M
over the arrows in the language generated by the original grammar
G
.
The pullback grammar admits the following concrete description in traditional CFG syntax:

•

its nonterminals are pairs
(R,(q,q^{\prime}))
– which for geometric intuition we prefer to visualize as triples
(q,R,q^{\prime})
– for every nonterminal
R
of the grammar
G
and pair of states
q,q^{\prime}
of the automaton
M
such that
p_{G}(R)=(p_{M}(q),p_{M}(q^{\prime}))
;

•

it has a production rule
(q,R,q^{\prime})\to\alpha_{0}(q_{1},R_{1},q^{\prime}_{1})\alpha_{1}\dots(q_{n},R_{n},q^{\prime}_{n})\alpha_{n}

for every production rule
R\to w_{0}R_{1}w_{1}\dots R_{n}w_{n}
of
G

and sequence of
n+1
runs
\alpha_{0}:q\to q_{1}
,
\alpha_{1}:q^{\prime}_{1}\to q_{2}
,
\dots
,
\alpha_{n}:q^{\prime}_{n}\to q^{\prime}
of
M
over the respective arrows
w_{0},\dots,w_{n}
;

•

the start symbol is
(q_{0},S,q_{f})
, where
S
is the start symbol of
G
and
q_{0}
and
q_{f}
are the initial and accepting states of
M
.

{exa}

Recall from Example
9
that to any arrow
w:A\to B
of a category with finitary factorizations
\mathcal{C}
, there is associated an NFA
M_{w}
recognizing exactly the singleton language
\{\,{w}\,\}
.
If
G
is a CFG over
\mathcal{C}
, then by pulling back
G
along
M_{w}
we obtain a new grammar
\mathop{M_{w}^{*}}G
that may be seen as a specialization of
G
to the factors of
w
.
In particular, in the classical case where
w=a_{1}\dots a_{n}
is a word of length
n
over
\Sigma
, the nonterminals of
\mathop{M_{w}^{*}}G
may be taken as triples
(i,R,j)
of a nonterminal
R
of
G
together with a pair of indices
0\leq i,j\leq n
, generating all the parses of the subword
w_{i,j}=a_{i+1}\dots a_{j}
as an
R
.
If we then view this grammar as defining a displayed operad over
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathsf{Fact}_{w}}
, as discussed in §
1.5
–§
1.6
, we obtain precisely the classical parse matrices
N_{i,j}
referenced in §
1.7
.

As an immediate corollary of Theorem
33
, we obtain the closure of CFLs of arrows under intersection with regular languages of arrows.

Corollary 34
.

If
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(A,B)
is context-free and
\mathcal{L}_{M}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(A,B)
is regular then
\mathcal{L}\cap\mathcal{L}_{M}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(A,B)
is context-free.

Proof 3.4
.

By taking the functorial image (Prop.
5
(3)) of the CFL
p_{M}^{-1}(\mathcal{L}_{G})\cap\mathcal{Q}(q_{0},q_{f})
along the functor
p_{M}
.

Explicitly, the grammar generating
L\cap L_{M}
is defined by starting with the pullback grammar
G^{\prime}=\mathop{M^{*}}G
from Theorem
33
above and postcomposing the functor
p^{\prime}_{G}:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{Q}}
with
\mathcal{W}\,{p_{M}}:\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{Q}}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
.
This grammar admits almost the same concrete description as
G^{\prime}
but where the production rules instead take the form

(q,R,q^{\prime})\to w_{0}(q_{1},R_{1},q^{\prime}_{1})w_{1}\dots(q_{n},R_{n},q^{\prime}_{n})w_{n}

for every production rule
R\to w_{0}R_{1}w_{1}\dots R_{n}w_{n}
of
G

and sequence of
n+1
runs
q\to q_{1}
,
q^{\prime}_{1}\to q_{2}
,
\dots
,
q^{\prime}_{n}\to q^{\prime}
of
M
over the respective arrows
w_{0},\dots,w_{n}
.
The reader familiar with the classic “Bar-Hillel construction” for combining a CFG with an NFA
[
BHPS61
, Theorem 8.1]
may recognize that our construction is very similar, but completely uniform and with the benefit of being derived systematically.

Although it will not be relevant to the representation theorem for categorical CFGs, let us observe that the construction of the pullback of a CFG along a categorical NFA works just as well to construct the pullback of a generalized CFG (in the sense of §
1.8
) along an operadic NFA.

Theorem 35
.

For any gCFG
G=(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{S},S,p_{G})
and operadic NFA
M=(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{Q},p_{M},q_{r})
over the same operad, with
p_{G}(S)=p_{M}(q_{r})
, there is a gCFG
G^{\prime}=\mathop{M^{*}}G
generating the language
\mathcal{L}_{G^{\prime}}=p_{M}^{-1}(\mathcal{L}_{G})\cap\mathcal{Q}(q_{r})
.

Indeed, the proof is almost identical to the proof of Theorem
33
, simply omitting the first step where we applied the functor
\mathcal{W}
before applying Lemma
31
.
We immediately obtain that gCFLs are closed under intersection with regular languages of constants.

Corollary 36
.

If
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(A)
is a gCFL and
\mathcal{L}_{M}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(A)
is regular then
L\cap\mathcal{L}_{M}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(A)
is a gCFL.

3.2.
The contour category of an operad and the contour / splicing adjunction

In §
1.2
, we began by explaining how to construct a functor

\mathcal{W}:\mathrm{Cat}\to\mathrm{Oper}

transforming any category
\mathcal{C}
into an operad
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
of spliced arrows of arbitrary arity, which played a central role in our definition of context-free language of arrows in a category.
We construct now a left adjoint functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Oper}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}}

(10)

which extracts from any given operad
\mathcal{O}
a category
\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{O}}
whose arrows may be interpreted as “oriented contours” along the boundary of the operations of the operad.

{defi}

The
contour category
\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{O}}
of an operad
\mathcal{O}
is defined as a quotient of the following free category:

•

objects are given by
oriented colors

R^{\epsilon}
consisting of a color
R
of
\mathcal{O}
and an orientation
\epsilon\in\{\,{u,d}\,\}
(“up” or “down”);

•

arrows are generated by pairs
(f,i)
of an operation
f:R_{1},\dots,R_{n}\to R
of
\mathcal{O}
and an index
0\leq i\leq n
, defining an arrow
R_{i}^{d}\to R_{i+1}^{u}
under the conventions that
R_{0}^{d}=R^{u}
and
R_{n+1}^{u}=R^{d}
;

subject to the conditions that
id_{R^{u}}=(id_{R},0)
and
id_{R^{d}}=(id_{R},1)
as well as the following equations:

\displaystyle(f\circ_{i}g,j)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(f,j)&\text{if }j<i\\
(f,i)(g,0)\hphantom{(f,i+1)}&\text{if }j=i\\
(g,j-i)&\text{if }i<j<i+m\\
(g,m)(f,i+1)&\text{if }j=i+m\\
(f,j-m+1)&\text{if }j>i+m\end{cases}

(11)

\displaystyle(f\circ_{i}c,j)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(f,j)&\text{if }j<i\\
(f,i)(c,0)(f,i+1)&\text{if }j=i\\
(f,j+1)&\text{if }j>i\end{cases}

(12)

whenever the left-hand side is well-formed,
for every operation
f
, operation
g
of positive arity
m>0
, constant
c
,
and indices
i
and
j
in the appropriate range.

We refer to each generating arrow
(f,i)
of the contour category
\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{O}}
as a
sector
of the operation
f
.
See Fig.
5
for a graphical interpretation of sectors and of the equations on contours seen as compositions of sectors.

Figure 5.
Left: interpretation of the generating arrows of the contour category
\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{O}}
. Right: interpretation of equations (
11
) and (
12
).

The contour construction provides a left adjoint to the spliced arrow construction
because a functor of operads

\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}

is entirely described by the data of a pair of objects
(A,B)=(R^{u},R^{d})
in
\mathcal{C}
for every color
R
in
\mathcal{O}
together with a sequence
f_{0},f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
of
n+1
arrows in
\mathcal{C}
, where
f_{i}:R_{i}^{d}\to R_{i+1}^{u}
for
0\leq i\leq n
for each operation
f:R_{1},\dots,R_{n}\to R
of
\mathcal{O}
, under the same conventions as above.
The equations (
11
) and (
12
) on the generators of
\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{O}}
reflect the equations imposed by the functor of operads
\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
on the spliced arrows of
\mathcal{C}
appearing as the image of operations in
\mathcal{O}
.
In that way we transform any functor of operads
\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
into a functor

\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{O}}\to\mathcal{C}

which may be seen as an interpretion of the contours of the operations of
\mathcal{O}
in
\mathcal{C}
.

The unit and counit of the contour / splicing adjunction also have nice descriptions.
The unit of the adjunction defines, for any operad
\mathcal{O}
, a functor of operads
\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{O}}}
that acts on colors by
R\mapsto(R^{u},R^{d})
,
and on operations by sending an operation
f:R_{1},\dots,R_{n}\to R
of
\mathcal{O}
to the spliced word of sectors
(f,0){-}\dots{-}(f,n):(R_{1}^{u},R_{1}^{d}),\dots,(R_{n}^{u},R_{n}^{d})\to(R^{u},R^{d})
.
The counit of the adjunction defines, for any category
\mathcal{C}
, a functor of categories
\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}}\to\mathcal{C}
that acts on objects by
(A,B)^{u}\mapsto A
and
(A,B)^{d}\mapsto B
, and on arrows by sending the
i
th sector of a spliced word to its
i
th word,
(w_{0}{-}\dots{-}w_{n},i)\mapsto w_{i}
.

Remark 37
.

In the case of a free operad over a species
\mathbb{S}
,
the contour category
\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}

admits an even simpler description as a free category
\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}\cong\mathcal{F}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathbb{S}}}
generated by the arrows
(x,i):R_{i}^{d}\to R_{i+1}^{u}
for every node
x:R_{1},\dots,R_{n}\to R
of the species
\mathbb{S}
.
Indeed, it is easy to verify that there is an analogous adjunction between species and graphs, and that this pair of contour / splicing adjunctions commutes with the pair of respective free / forgetful adjunctions:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Oper}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spec}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Grph}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

(13)

We refer to the generating arrows
(x,i)
of the contour graph
\mathcal{C}\,{\mathbb{S}}
as
corners
in the sense of the theory of planar maps
[
Sch15
]
, since geometrically they indeed correspond to the corners of
\mathbb{S}
-rooted trees seen as rooted planar maps.
By (
13
), every sector of an operation of
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
factors uniquely in the contour category
\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}
as a sequence of corners.

In contrast to the situation for
\mathcal{W}
(Prop.
19
), it is
not
the case that
\mathcal{C}
always preserves the ULF property.

Remark 38
.

Consider the category
\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}
with two objects
A
and
B
and only identity arrows,
and the unique functor
p
to the terminal category
\mathbf{1}
.
We claim that the associated ULF functor of operads
\mathcal{W}\,{p}
induces a functor of categories
\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{W}\,{p}}
which is not ULF.
Consider the two binary operations
f=id_{A}{-}id_{A}{-}id_{A}
and
g=id_{A}{-}id_{A}{-}id_{B}

and the constant
c=id_{A}
in
\mathcal{W}\,{(\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1})}
,
as well as the binary operations
h=id_{\ast}{-}id_{\ast}{-}id_{\ast}
and the constant
d=id_{\ast}

in
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathbf{1}}
.
The category
\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{W}\,{(\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1})}}
has the sequence of sectors
\alpha=(f,0)(c,0)(g,1)
as an arrow, which
is different from the identity. On the other hand, it is mapped by
\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{W}\,{p}}
to the sequence
w=(h,0)(d,0)(h,1)
, which is equal thanks to Equation (
12
)
to the sector
(h\circ_{0}d,0)
of the unary operation
h\circ_{0}d=id_{\ast}{-}id_{\ast}
of
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathbf{1}}
,
and hence
w=id_{(\ast,\ast)^{u}}
.
Since the factorization
id=id\,id
in
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathbf{1}}
lifts to two distinct factorizations
\alpha=id\,\alpha=\alpha\,id
in
\mathcal{W}\,{(\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1})}
,
p
is not ULF.

Still, we can verify that maps of species induce ULF functors between their contour categories, as an immediate consequence of Prop.
12
and the factorization (
13
).

Proposition 39
.

If
\psi:\mathbb{S}\to\mathbb{T}
is a map of species, then
\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\psi}:\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}\to\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{T}}
is a ULF functor of categories.

3.3.
The universal context-free grammar of a pointed species, and its associated tree contour language

Every finite species
\mathbb{S}
equipped with a color
S

comes with a
universal
context-free grammar
(\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}},\mathbb{S},S,p_{\mathbb{S}})
, characterized by the fact that
p_{\mathbb{S}}:{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}\to{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}}}
is the unit of the contour / splicing conjunction.
We denote the universal grammar associated to a pointed species
\dot{\mathbb{S}}:=(\mathbb{S},S)
by
\mathsf{Univ}_{\dot{\mathbb{S}}}
.
By “universal” context-free grammar, we mean that any categorical context-free grammar

G=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S},S,p)
with the same underlying species and start symbol factors uniquely through
\mathsf{Univ}_{\dot{\mathbb{S}}}
in the sense
that there exists a unique functor
q_{G}:\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}\to\mathcal{C}
satisfying the equation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}\hskip 8.19447pt=\hskip 8.19447pt{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathbb{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}\,{q_{G}}}

(14)

We refer to the language of arrows of a universal grammar
\mathsf{Univ}_{\dot{\mathbb{S}}}

as the
tree contour language
associated to a pointed species, denoted
\mathcal{C\!L}_{\dot{\mathbb{S}}}
, and to its arrows as
tree contour words
, or more precisely as
\dot{\mathbb{S}}
-tree contour words.
Tree contour words in
\mathcal{C\!L}_{\dot{\mathbb{S}}}
describe the contours of
\mathbb{S}
-rooted trees with root color
S
, see left side of Fig.
6
for an illustration.

Figure 6.
Left: an
\mathbb{S}
-rooted tree of root color 1 and its corresponding contour word
\mathsf{a_{0}b_{0}a_{1}c_{0}d_{0}c_{1}e_{0}c_{2}a_{2}f_{0}g_{0}f_{1}a_{3}}:1^{u}\to 1^{d}
.
Right: the corresponding Dyck word obtained by splitting each corner of the contour in two pieces.

The factorization above shows that any context-free grammar
G
is the functorial image of the universal grammar
\mathsf{Univ}_{\dot{\mathbb{S}}}
along the functor of categories
q_{G}
, whose purpose is to transport each corner of a node in
\mathbb{S}
to the corresponding arrow in
\mathcal{C}
as determined by
G
.
At the level of languages, we have

Proposition 40
.

The language of a context-free grammar
G
coincides with the image of its tree contour language,
\mathcal{L}_{G}=q_{G}\,\mathcal{C\!L}_{\dot{\mathbb{S}}}
.

{exa}

Consider the context-free grammar of Figure
2
.
The production rules of the universal grammar
\mathsf{Univ}_{\dot{\mathbb{S}}}
may be depicted in traditional CFG syntax as

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}

\displaystyle\to 1_{0}\,\mathsf{NP}\,1_{1}\,\mathsf{VP}\,1_{2}

\displaystyle\mathsf{NP}

\displaystyle\to 2_{0}

\displaystyle\mathsf{NP}

\displaystyle\to 3_{0}

\displaystyle\mathsf{VP}

\displaystyle\to 4_{0}\,\mathsf{NP}\,4_{1}

where we have written
i_{j}
as shorthand for the sector
(x_{i},j)
.
The language
\mathcal{L}_{G}
is then obtained as the image of
\mathcal{C\!L}_{\dot{\mathbb{S}}}
under the functor
q_{G}:\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}\to\mathcal{F}\,{\mathbb{B}_{\Sigma}}
defined by:

1_{0}\mapsto id\quad 1_{1}\mapsto\text{\textvisiblespace}\quad 1_{2}\mapsto id

2_{0}\mapsto\mathsf{mom}\qquad 3_{0}\mapsto\mathsf{tom}\qquad 4_{0}\mapsto\mathsf{loves}\text{\textvisiblespace}\quad 4_{1}\mapsto id

Remark 41
.

The notion of tree contour language makes sense even for infinite species, although in that case the resulting universal grammar
\mathsf{Univ}_{\dot{\mathbb{S}}}
is no longer a CFG, having infinitely many nonterminals or infinitely many productions.
Still, it may be an interesting object of study.
In particular, the tree contour language
\mathsf{Univ}_{(\mathbb{N},\ast)}
generated by the
terminal species

\mathbb{N}
with one color
\ast
and a single operation of every arity appears to be of combinatorial interest, with words in the language describing the shapes of rooted planar trees with arbitrary node degrees.
Nevertheless, from now on when we refer to a “tree contour language” we will always mean the context-free tree contour language generated by a finite pointed species.

3.4.
Representation theorem

The achievement of the classical Chomsky-Schützenberger representation theorem is to separate any
context-free grammar
G=(\Sigma,N,S,P)

into two independent components:

(1)

a context-free grammar
G^{\prime}
with only one nonterminal over an alphabet

\Sigma_{2k}=\{\,{[_{1},]_{1},\dots,[_{k},]_{k}}\,\}

of size
2k
, for some
k
, which generates Dyck words of balanced brackets describing the shapes of parse trees with nodes labelled by
production rules of
G
; and

(2)

a finite-state automaton
M
to check that the edges of these trees may be appropriately colored by
the nonterminals of
G
according to the labels of the nodes specifying the productions.

The original context-free language generated by
G
is then obtained as the image of the intersection of the
Dyck language generated by
G^{\prime}
with the regular language recognized by
M
, under a homomorphism
\Sigma_{2k}^{*}\to\Sigma^{*}
that interprets
each bracket of the Dyck word by a word in the original alphabet, with some arbitrary choice involved in selecting whether to interpret opening or closing brackets as empty words.

In this section, we give a new proof of the representation theorem,
generalized to context-free grammars
G
over any category
\mathcal{C}
.
Since the category
\mathcal{C}
may have more than one object, the appropriate statement of the representation theorem cannot require the grammar describing the shapes of parse trees to have only one nonterminal.
Nonetheless, we can construct one that is
\mathcal{C}
-chromatic
in the following sense.

{defi}

A categorical context-free grammar
G=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S},S,p)
is
\mathcal{C}
-chromatic

if the functor
p:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}
is injective on colors.

The nonterminals of a
\mathcal{C}
-chromatic grammar may thus be considered as pairs
(A,B)
of objects of
\mathcal{C}
.

Moreover, rather than using Dyck words to represent parse trees, we find it more natural to use tree contour words, based on the observation given above in §
3.3

that every context-free language may be
canonically
represented as the image of a tree contour language generated by a context-free grammar with the same set of nonterminals.
(We will discuss the relationship between contour words and Dyck words as Remark
45
below.)

As preparation to our proof of the representation theorem, we establish:

Proposition 42
.

Any map of species
\phi:\mathbb{S}\to\mathbb{T}
factors as
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\hskip 8.19447pt=\hskip 8.19447pt\mathbb{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{C}\,\mathbb{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{{\mathsf{colors}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\mathsf{nodes}}}

for some species
\phi_{C}\,\mathbb{S}
such that
\phi_{\mathsf{colors}}
is the identity on nodes and surjective on colors and
\phi_{\mathsf{nodes}}
is injective on colors.

Proof 3.5
.

A map of species is given by a commutative diagram as on the right below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathbb{S}}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\mathbb{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathbb{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathbb{T}}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\mathbb{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\mathbb{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{C}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o^{\prime}}

After taking the epi-mono factorization of
\phi_{C}
,

we obtain the desired factorization of
\phi
:
