Lemma 43
.

Let
\psi:\mathbb{S}\to\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
be a map of species, let
p_{\mathbb{S}}:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}}
and
p_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}:\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}}
be the underlying functors of the universal grammars derived from the unit of the contour / splicing adjunction, and let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathbb{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\,\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\psi}}}

(15)

be the associated naturality square.
If
\psi
is injective on nodes then the canonical functor of operads from
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}
to the pullback of
p_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}
along
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\psi}}
is fully faithful.

Proof 3.6
.

The naturality square (
15
) in
\mathrm{Oper}
induces a commutative square in
\mathrm{Spec}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\mathbb{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\psi}}}

(16)

Neither (
15
) nor (
16
) will in general be a pullback square.
However, by Lemma
31
, we know that the pullback of
p_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}
along
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\psi}}

is obtained from a corresponding pullback in the category of species

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\psi}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\,\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\psi}}}

(17)

where the pullback species
\mathbb{T}
has colors defined as tuples
(R,(R_{1}^{u},R_{2}^{d}))

of a color
R
of
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
and colors
R_{1}
,
R_{2}
of
\mathbb{S}
such that
{\psi(R_{1})=\psi(R_{2})=R}
, and
where the
n
-ary nodes of
\mathbb{R}
are defined as pairs
(x,f)

of a
n
-ary node
x
of
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
and an
n
-ary operation
f
of
\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}}
,
necessarily of the form
f=(y,0){-}\dots{-}(y,n)

for
y
the unique
n
-ary node of
\mathbb{S}
such that
\psi(y)=x
,
by assumption that
\psi:\mathbb{S}\to\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
is injective on nodes.
The canonical map of species
{\mathbb{S}}\to{\mathbb{T}}
induced by (
16
) and the left-hand side of (
17
) transports every color
R
of
\mathbb{S}
to the color
((\psi(R)^{u},\psi(R)^{d}),R)

and every
n
-ary node
y
of
\mathbb{S}
to the
n
-ary node
(\psi(y),(y,0){-}\dots{-}(y,n))
of
\mathbb{T}
.
From this follows that the canonical map of species
{\mathbb{S}}\to{\mathbb{T}}
is injective on colors and bijective on nodes.
Moreover, there are no nodes in
\mathbb{T}
whose colors are outside of the image of
\mathbb{S}
.
We conclude that the canonical functor of operads
\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}\to\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{T}
induced by (
15
) and the right-hand side of (
17
) is fully faithful.

Now, let
G=(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{S},S,p)
be any categorical context-free grammar, and using Prop.
42
consider the corresponding
\mathcal{C}
-chromatic grammar
G_{\mathsf{nodes}}=(\mathcal{C},\phi_{C}\,\mathbb{S},(A,B),p_{\mathsf{nodes}})
, where
p(S)=(A,B)
.
Applying the factorization (
14
), we build the commutative diagram below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}\,\phi_{C}\,\mathbb{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\phi_{C}\,\mathbb{S}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathbb{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\,\phi_{\mathsf{colors}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\phi_{C}\,\mathbb{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}\,{\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\phi_{\mathsf{colors}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}\,{q_{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}\,{q_{G_{\mathsf{nodes}}}}}

(18)

combining a naturality square with a triangle whose commutativity follows from equation
\phi=\phi_{\mathsf{colors}}\,\phi_{\mathsf{nodes}}

and the contour / splicing adjunction.
Note also that
p_{\mathsf{colors}}:=\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\phi_{\mathsf{colors}}}
is a ULF functor of categories by Prop.
39
, and finitary
because
\phi_{\mathsf{colors}}
is finitary (and even finite).
Therefore pairing the functor with the initial state
S^{u}
and accepting state
S^{d}
defines a finite-state automaton
M_{\mathsf{colors}}=(\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\phi_{C}\,\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{C}\,{\mathcal{F}\,\mathbb{S}},p_{\mathsf{colors}},S^{u},S^{d})
.
By the intersection construction (Cor.
34
) together with Lemma
43
and the strong translation principle (Prop.
6
),
we deduce that

p_{\mathsf{colors}}\,\mathcal{C\!L}_{\dot{\mathbb{S}}}\,=\,\mathcal{C\!L}_{\phi_{C}\,\dot{\mathbb{S}}}\,\cap\,\mathcal{L}_{M_{\mathsf{colors}}}\,.

Finally, using that
G
is the image of the universal grammar
\mathsf{Univ}_{\dot{\mathbb{S}}}
and considering the commutative diagram (
18
), we conclude that

\mathcal{L}_{G}=q_{G}\,\mathcal{C\!L}_{\dot{\mathbb{S}}}=q_{G_{\mathsf{nodes}}}\,p_{\mathsf{colors}}\,\mathcal{C\!L}_{\dot{\mathbb{S}}}=q_{G_{\mathsf{nodes}}}\,(\mathcal{C\!L}_{\phi_{C}\,\dot{\mathbb{S}}}\,\cap\,\mathcal{L}_{M_{\mathsf{colors}}})

(19)

and thus derive our generalized Chomsky-Schützenberger representation theorem.

Theorem 44
.

A language
L\subseteq\mathcal{C}(A,B)
is a CFL of arrows iff it is a functorial image of the intersection of a
\mathcal{C}
-chromatic tree contour language with a regular language.

{exa}

For the original example of Figure
2
, the colors-nodes factorization
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}\,{\Sigma}\hskip 8.19447pt=\hskip 8.19447pt\mathbb{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{C}\,\mathbb{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}\,{\Sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{{\mathsf{colors}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\mathsf{nodes}}}

of Prop.
42
may be visualized as below:

Some examples of contour words in the tree contour language
\mathcal{C\!L}_{\phi_{C}\,\dot{\mathbb{S}}}
and their corresponding images under the functor
q_{G_{\mathsf{nodes}}}
include:

Observe that only the first contour word encodes a valid parse tree mapping to a word of the language.
Finally, the “coloring automaton”
M_{\mathsf{colors}}
derived by the contour construction may be depicted by the following transition diagram:

\mathsf{S}^{u}
\mathsf{NP}^{u}
\mathsf{VP}^{u}
\mathsf{NP}^{d}
\mathsf{VP}^{d}
\mathsf{S}^{d}
\mathsf{1_{0}}
\mathsf{2_{0}}
\mathsf{3_{0}}
\mathsf{1_{1}}
\mathsf{4_{0}}
\mathsf{4_{1}}
\mathsf{1_{2}}

The reader can verify that the automaton only accepts the first contour word above.

Remark 45
.

We view the use of contour words rather than Dyck words in Theorem
44
as a mild improvement of the original theorem by Chomsky and Schützenberger, since it removes the need for choosing arbitrarily whether to erase left brackets or right brackets in defining the homomorphism.
However, there is a close relationship between tree contour words and Dyck words, and the original representation can be recovered from formula (
19
).
We now sketch this for the classical case of context-free languages of words over an alphabet
\Sigma
. As suggested by the right-hand side of Figure
6
, there is a simple mapping
s
(for
subdivide
) from contour words to Dyck words defined by translating each corner
(x,i)
of an
n
-ary node
x
into a pair of brackets, as follows:

s\quad=\quad(x,i)\mapsto\begin{cases}[^{x}_{0}\ ]^{x}_{0}&\text{if }i=n=0\\
[^{x}_{0}\ [^{x}_{1}&\text{if }i=0\text{ and }n>0\\
]^{x}_{i}\ [^{x}_{i+1}&\text{if }0<i<n\\
]^{x}_{n}\ ]^{x}_{0}&\text{if }0<i=n\end{cases}

It is easy to check that the image of a tree contour word under this translation is a well-bracketed word over the alphabet containing a pair of brackets
[^{x}_{i}
and
]^{x}_{i}
for every
n
-ary node
x
and index
0\leq i\leq n
.
Indeed, every production rule

R\to(x,0)\>R_{1}\>(x,1)\dots R_{n}\>(x,n)

of the universal grammar
\mathsf{Univ}_{\dot{\mathbb{S}}}
is translated to a production rule

R\to[^{x}_{0}\>[^{x}_{1}\>R_{1}\>]^{x}_{1}\>[^{x}_{2}\cdots R_{n}\>]^{x}_{n}\>]^{x}_{0}

(20)

which manifestly preserves well-bracketing.
Let us refer to the language of well-bracketed words generated by the collection of productions (
20
), indexed by nodes
x:R_{1},\dots,R_{n}\to R
of a species
\mathbb{S}
with start symbol
S\in\mathbb{S}
, as the
\dot{\mathbb{S}}
-Dyck language

\mathcal{D\!L}_{\dot{\mathbb{S}}}
.
By construction, it is immediate that

s(\mathcal{C\!L}_{\dot{\mathbb{S}}})=\mathcal{D\!L}_{\dot{\mathbb{S}}}

(21)

for every pointed species
\dot{\mathbb{S}}
.
Conversely, there are two natural ways to define a translation from
\dot{\mathbb{S}}
-Dyck words back to
\dot{\mathbb{S}}
-contour words: the “green” translation

g\quad=\quad[^{x}_{i}\ \mapsto\begin{cases}(x,0)&\text{if }i=0\\
\epsilon&\text{if }i>0\end{cases}\qquad]^{x}_{i}\mapsto\begin{cases}\epsilon&\text{if }i=0\\
(x,i)&\text{if }i>0\end{cases}\quad,

which corresponds to interpreting the green arrows in the right hand side of Figure
6
as corners and erasing the red arrows,
or alternatively the “red” translation

r\quad=\quad[^{x}_{i}\ \mapsto\begin{cases}\epsilon&\text{if }i=0\\
(x,i)&\text{if }i>0\end{cases}\qquad]^{x}_{i}\mapsto\begin{cases}(x,0)&\text{if }i=0\\
\epsilon&\text{if }i>0\end{cases}\quad,

which does the opposite.
Both translations are left inverse to the
s
-translation in the sense that
g\circ s=r\circ s=id
.
It follows that if we define the homomorphism
h
from bracketed words to
\Sigma^{*}
to be either
h=q_{G_{\mathsf{nodes}}}\circ g
or
h=q_{G_{\mathsf{nodes}}}\circ r
, then we can derive that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{G}

\displaystyle=q_{G_{\mathsf{nodes}}}\,(\mathcal{C\!L}_{\phi_{C}\,\dot{\mathbb{S}}}\,\cap\,\mathcal{L}_{M_{\mathsf{colors}}})

by
(
19
)

\displaystyle=h\,(s\,(\mathcal{C\!L}_{\phi_{C}\,\dot{\mathbb{S}}}\,\cap\,\mathcal{L}_{M_{\mathsf{colors}}}))

\displaystyle\text{since }g\circ s=r\circ s=id

\displaystyle=h\,(\mathcal{D\!L}_{\phi_{C}\,\dot{\mathbb{S}}}\,\cap\,s(\mathcal{L}_{M_{\mathsf{colors}}}))

\displaystyle\text{by }\eqref{s-contour-is-S-dyck}\text{ and injectivity of }s.

(22)

Next, it is not hard to verify that the image
s(\mathcal{L}_{M_{\mathsf{colors}}})
of the regular language of the coloring automaton under the
s
-translation is itself a regular language (just use a pair of transitions to simulate each transition of
M_{\mathsf{colors}}
).
We have therefore derived that every context-free language is a homomorphic image of the intersection of a
\phi_{C}\,\dot{\mathbb{S}}
-Dyck language (for some
\dot{\mathbb{S}}
) with a regular language.

Words in
\mathcal{D\!L}_{\phi_{C}\,\dot{\mathbb{S}}}
are generated by productions of the form

S\to[^{x}_{0}\>[^{x}_{1}\>S\>]^{x}_{1}\>[^{x}_{2}\cdots S\>]^{x}_{n}\>]^{x}_{0}

(23)

for every node
x:R_{1},\dots,R_{n}\to R
in
\mathbb{S}
(i.e., replacing every non-terminal in (
20
) by the unique start symbol
S
).
Now let
k=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{S}}1+|x|
be the sum of the arity-plus-one of every node in
\mathbb{S}
, and let us write
\mathcal{D\!L}_{k}
for the language of unrestricted Dyck words over the family of
k
different pairs of brackets labelled
[^{x}_{i}
and
]^{x}_{i}
for
0\leq i\leq n=|x|
as above.
One can prove by induction that an arbitrary Dyck word is a
(\phi_{C}\,\dot{\mathbb{S}})
-Dyck word just in case it satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

it starts with
[^{x}_{0}
for some
x
;

(2)

every
[^{x}_{0}
is immediately followed by either
]^{x}_{0}
(if
|x|=0
) or
[^{x}_{1}
(if
|x|>0
);

(3)

for
i>0
, every
[^{x}_{i}
is followed by an opening bracket
[^{y}_{0}
for some
y
;

(4)

for
i>0
, every
]^{x}_{i}
is immediately followed by either
[^{x}_{i+1}
(if
i<|x|
) or
]^{x}_{0}
(if
i=|x|
);

(5)

]^{x}_{0}
is never followed by an opening bracket
[^{y}_{i}
.

Since these conditions can be checked by a simple finite automaton
M_{\mathsf{index}}
, we have that

\mathcal{D\!L}_{\phi_{C}\,\dot{\mathbb{S}}}=\mathcal{D\!L}_{k}\cap\mathcal{L}_{M_{\mathsf{index}}}

(24)

from which the Chomsky-Schützenberger representation theorem follows by substituting (
24
) into (
22
) and using that regular languages are closed under intersection.

Although we have limited our attention here to the classical result, we believe that the connection between contour words and Dyck words deserves a deeper categorical analysis, which we leave to future work.
The
s
-translation has a clear geometric interpretation as decomposing each corner of the contour into alternating actions of walking along an edge and turning around a node (we again refer the reader to Figure
6
).
In the theory of maps on surfaces, an analogous decomposition is used to embed the
oriented cartographic group
into the
unoriented cartographic group
(cf.
[
SV90
,
JS94
]
), which suggests that there may be a corresponding refinement of the contour category construction.

3.5.
Related work

As already mentioned, our proof that (generalized) context-free languages are closed under intersection with regular languages may be seen as a decomposition of the Bar-Hillel construction
[
BHPS61
]
.
In a similar spirit, Ludmann, Pogodalla, and De Groote have recently analyzed Kanazawa’s proof that languages generated by abstract categorial grammars are closed under intersection with regular languages, and shown that it defines a pullback in an appropriate category
[
LPdG22
]
.

The representation theorem is one of many results appearing in Chomsky and Schützenberger’s joint article on “The algebraic theory of context-free languges”
[
CS63
]
.
The theorem was sketched in a technical report by Chomsky
[
Cho62
]
with attribution to their collaboration, while the first detailed proof seems to have been by Schützenberger
[
Sch63
]
.
The original formulation used
two-sided
6
6

6

The two-sided Dyck language on an alphabet of
2n
letters
x_{\pm i}

(1\leq i\leq n)
may be defined as the kernel of the canonical homomorphism
\phi:\mathrm{Mon}\langle x_{-n},\dots,x_{-1},x_{+1},\dots,x_{+n}\rangle\to\mathrm{Grp}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle
from the free monoid on
2n
generators to the free group on
n
generators defined by
\phi(x_{+i})=x_{i}
and
\phi(x_{-i})=x_{i}^{-1}
.
Dyck languages, but would have worked just as well with ordinary (one-sided) Dyck languages and most standard treatments of the theorem now adopt that formulation
[
Koz97
, §G]
.
Numerous variations of the representation theorem and extensions to other classes of languages have since been established, see
[
CS16
]
for an overview and bibliography.

The contour / splicing adjunction is fundamental to our analysis of the representation theorem, providing an unexpected geometric lens on context-free languages that is evocative of Girard’s geometry of interaction. Although the adjunction is not identified, this geometric perspective is also apparent in Slavnov’s recent work
[
Sla20
]
(inspired both by abstract categorial grammars and by linear logic proof-nets), wherein he constructs a compact closed monoidal category of
word cobordisms
reminiscent of the operad of spliced words.

After the original version of this article was published
[
MZ22
]
, the contour / splicing adjunction was subsequently analyzed and extended by Earnshaw, Hefford, and Román, who described applications to process algebra
[
EHR24
,
Rom23
]
.
They noticed that the splicing functor
\mathcal{W}:\mathrm{Cat}\to\mathrm{Oper}
is closely related to a construction by Day and Street
[
DS04
, Example 7.3]
, of a canonical promonoidal structure on
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes\mathcal{C}
for any
\mathcal{V}
-enriched category
\mathcal{C}
.
We have also been further exploring the 2-categorical aspects of the adjunction between categories and operads in ongoing work with Peter Faul.

4.
Conclusion

We have seen that the classical notions of context-free grammar and of nondeterministic finite-state automaton can be reformulated and generalized in a categorical setting, in a way that seems to have significant explanatory power.
Clearly many natural questions arise in regards to integrating the deep body of work on automata theory and formal language theory within this setting, some of which we have already signalled.
Another obvious question is how to define pushdown automata over categories.
We find it remarkable that when one considers the nodes of a species as the production rules of a context-free grammar, the corners of its contour category correspond exactly to the
items
used in LR parsing and Earley parsing
[
Knu65
,
Ear70
]
, which we think suggests contour categories have a role to play in analyzing left-to-right parsing algorithms from a categorical perspective.
One of our original motivations for studying context-free grammars as functors and looking at parsing as a lifting problem was to develop a better understanding of the universal properties of algorithms like these, having in mind that such an understanding could then be transferred to other settings including type checking and type inference algorithms.
This attempt at unification is supported by the emergence of a number of categorical and fibrational structures which seem to connect these different kinds of symbolic systems, revealing that they are founded on many of the same basic patterns.
